# What hurts kernel developers: tight loops, pain points and workarounds in the current Linux and BSD kernels, compared with pdp64

[← pdp64 index](../README.md)

**Part II of the kernel assembly survey ([`kernel-asm-survey.md`](kernel-asm-survey.md)).** Part I counts the instructions a kernel hand-writes; this part asks why: which paths run so often that cycles and bytes matter, which hardware behaviours cost kernel developers the most effort, what they do about them, and how the pdp64 system design stands against each.

**Status: Phase 1 complete; Phase 2 (the lists) started.** Phase 1 reads only local sources: the current Linux, FreeBSD, NetBSD and OpenBSD source trees (shallow, sparse checkouts under `~/claude/kernel-src`, the commits named in Appendix B), the Linux `Documentation/` tree, the BSD section 9 manual pages, the old Unix sources in `~/claude/facetlang/unixtree`, and the pdp64 documents. Everything that needs history (when a pain started, how it changed over thirty years, what the mailing lists argued about) or the web (papers, articles, mailing-list archives) is **not done**: it is Phase 2, listed in Appendix C with the status "not yet". Statements from memory are labelled *unverified* and are not used as evidence.

**Evidence labels used throughout.** **[G] guarantee**: a manual, standard or the developers' own documentation states it. **[P] practice**: the current kernel code, a Kconfig help text or a count over the code shows it. **[S] secondary**: an article or wiki (none yet; Phase 2). **[U] unverified**: from memory, to be fetched. Every number says how it was measured, in the text or in Appendix B.

**The author's principles, in order, are the yardstick** (decisions-pending, Part 0): (a) code size, (b) easy recoverability, meaning fewer possible errors and less recovery code, (c) speed and efficiency through fewer gates and tighter hot loops. Verdicts: **DESIGNED AWAY** (the problem cannot arise), **MITIGATED BY DESIGN** (it arises in a smaller or cheaper form), **SAME PROBLEM**, **WORSE**, **UNKNOWN** (not yet defined, or depends on an open decision). Decision identifiers (P1 to P16, Q1 to Q15, R1 to R8, S1 to S6) point to [`decisions-pending.md`](decisions-pending.md); E-numbers to [`errata.md`](errata.md).

## 0. Overview: the major pain points, by area

This chapter is a single place to see what hurt kernel developers, in order of how long and how badly it hurt, and what pdp64 does about it. It is a summary of the rest of the document: every figure and name below comes from a row in chapters 3 to 14G or in Appendices A to A.5, which are the evidence; the last column says where. It was written at the end of round 5 (23 Oct 2026).

**How to read the columns.** *Felt by*: the developers or projects named in the sources. *Cost*: numbers only where a source gives them; "not measured in the sources" otherwise. *A different design*: what hardware, bus or instruction-set design would have avoided it; where that is my inference and not something a source says, it is marked (inferred). *pdp64*: **AVOIDED** (cannot arise in the design as specified), **MITIGATED** (arises but is cheaper or contained), **OPEN** (the manual does not decide it), **NOT APPLICABLE** (pdp64 has no such part). Statuses marked † were re-checked against `docs/manual/` in round 5 (section numbers are the manual's); **in round 6 every status in this chapter was re-checked** and the rows that changed are listed in 14J (C9); the manual's section numbers now stand in place of the old chapter numbers. **pdp64 has no silicon, so it has no errata of its own, which is not the same as having none** (chapter 1).

**Ranking.** Within each area the order is mine, by (a) years the pain lasted, (b) how many developers and subsystems it touched, (c) measured cost where there is one. Where the sources do not support a ranking the entries are in the order of the evidence.

### 0.1 Memory system and processors

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Kernel mapped in every user address space; after Meltdown the page tables had to be split | KAISER/PTI merged in Jan 2018 after the Meltdown disclosure; the 2003 "4G/4G" scheme was the first public separate kernel/user page-table design (Molnar, 8 Jul 2003) | Gleixner, Hansen, Linus, Villard (NetBSD SVS), Dolecek | NetBSD build: 4 min 13 s to 4 min 20 s with SVS (2.7%, Intel Core i5); LWN estimates "about 5%" (Dec 2017) and "30% or more" for some loads (Jan 2018); Hansen: global mappings recovered +1.71% on a Skylake with PCID, +23.9% on an Atom without | A hard user/kernel split in the address (the Meltdown paper's "hard-split bit") or tagged translation (inferred from the paper) | **OPEN**: pdp64 maps the kernel in every user space (D6); safe today because there is no speculation, exposed to a future out-of-order core (ch. 1) | ch. 3-5, 14D, 14E |
| 2 | Physical memory larger than the address window (highmem, PAE, 4G/4G) | highmem added 1999 (2.3.16), still being phased out in 2025; the 4G/4G split proposed July 2003 | Molnar, Bergmann, kernel developers | the 2003 4G/4G scheme "cost too much" (the ch. 5 row of this document); highmem "one of the least popular features" (Bergmann) | 64-bit addresses from the start | **AVOIDED**: 39-bit virtual, 40-bit physical, three-level table over 4 KB pages (6.8.4†) | 14D, 14F, A.5 142 |
| 3 | Locks that do not scale: the big kernel lock, ticket locks, `mmap_sem` | BKL on the to-do list almost since the lock was added (Corbet); removed in 2.6.39 (2011); ticket locks slowed a microbenchmark "by a factor of two" with one contender and "by as much as a factor of ten" with many; Gorman: replacing `mmap_sem` would be "the big kernel lock all over again" | Corbet, Rik van Riel, Piggin, Gorman | see left | A memory/lock hierarchy designed with per-resource locks (inferred) | **OPEN** beyond a few cores (R3); 2 to 4 cores snooping keeps the bounce small (ch. 6) | 14A-14B, 14F, A.5 140 |
| 4 | TLB flush cost and shootdown across cores | per-architecture flush rules for decades; NetBSD FFS paid one shootdown IPI per mapped block (Mar 2018) | Dolecek, Gleixner, arm64 maintainers | not measured in one number in the sources | Broadcast invalidation in hardware | **MITIGATED**: `TLBI`/`TLBIA` broadcast (6.4.7†, 6.7.6†); still one broadcast per unmap | ch. 5, 14B |
| 5 | Accessed/dirty bits maintained in software or racing with hardware | VAX `vmpage.c` simulated a reference bit; modern cores update bits atomically with races | VAX-era paging code; x86/arm64 maintainers | not measured | Hardware A/D with a defined atomic update | **SAME PROBLEM** by choice: A/D bits software-managed (6.8.5†) | ch. 5, 14 |
| 6 | Virtually indexed caches: aliasing | ARM VIVT/VIPT, MIPS, SPARC, PA-RISC; `cachetlb.rst`: "The biggest problem is that of virtual aliasing in the data cache"; 131 files under `arch/` call cache-flush hooks; 179 `arch/arm` lines mention aliasing | Russell King and the architecture maintainers | not measured | A physically tagged cache, or index bits within the page offset | **OPEN**: "a cache question, not an ISA question" (`pdp64-isa.md` 17.3; the manual's chapter 7.7 says nothing on aliasing); the rule should be written as an implementation requirement | ch. 7 |
| 7 | Small kernel stacks and silent overflow | stack grew to 16 KB on x86-64 in 3.15; virtually mapped stacks merged after a 2016 patch set | Lutomirski, Corbet, Horn | `clone()` about 1.5 µs slower with vmapped stacks (patch set figure); DMA from the stack breaks | Large stacks with a guard page from the start (inferred) | **OPEN**: no stack-size or guard-page rule yet | 14F, A.5 143-144 |
| 8 | NMI nesting: IRET unmasks NMIs | NMI handler could not fault or hit a breakpoint until a 2012 rework (Rostedt, LWN 7 Mar 2012); the nesting state machine is still in `nmi.c` | Rostedt, x86 maintainers | not measured; restricts NMI handlers to no `vmalloc` memory | A return instruction that does not end the masking | **OPEN**: the manual has priority 7 on entry (6.2.2†) but no NMI rule | 14F, A.5 145 |
| 9 | Vector and FPU state growth; lazy FPU leaks; AVX-512 downclock | state grew with each extension; signal-stack minimum 2 KB broke with AMX (`xstate.rst`); "AVX512 ... known to slow the max clock speed of the core" | x86 maintainers, user-space libraries | not measured | A state-size enumeration and opt-in allocation (what the kernel did for AMX) | **AVOIDED** for vector state: the pdp64 has no vector register file (8.1, first paragraph); the state of the scalar floating-point unit was not checked (round 6: chapter 8 does not describe it) | ch. 8, 14F |
| 10 | Non-uniform cores and CPU hotplug | Torvalds, 19 Feb 2018: "I'm not convinced we handle the case of hotplug CPU's with different CPU models correctly"; hybrid x86 cores 2024 (OpenBSD `cpu cores`) | Torvalds, Moerbeek, Henderson | not measured | Uniform cores | **OPEN**: 7.8.1 releases cores by a per-core start register and treats them alike; the manual has no text on unlike cores or hotplug (round 6 check; round 5 had called this AVOIDED) | 14B, 14D |
| 11 | Compatibility layers and dead architectures | 591 lines of `compat` vocabulary; x32; Itanium dropped after 6.6 LTS (Biesheuvel, 11 Sep 2023) | Seibold (vDSO: 23 versions), Biesheuvel | vDSO patch 23 versions | No second ABI | **AVOIDED**: compatibility by conversion instructions, never by mode bits (9.2.5†) | ch. 11, 14B, 14F, A.5 |
| 12 | Hidden register machinery: SPARC register windows, MIPS software TLB refill | `wof.S`: "this is dirty and messy as all hell ... DON'T LOOK!"; MIPS refill handlers synthesised at boot | SPARC and MIPS maintainers | not measured | Plain registers; hardware walker (inferred) | **AVOIDED** for windows (sixteen ordinary registers, 9.3.1†); software refill is decision Q2 | 14F, A.5 148-149 |
| 13 | Alignment, byte order and unaligned access | Alpha unaligned-access rows (ch. 11); split-lock detection (over 1000 cycles) | Alpha, x86 maintainers | split lock "takes over 1000 extra cycles" | Architectural alignment with a defined fault | **AVOIDED**: quad data accesses are naturally aligned (9.2.11†); the abort doubles as the tag check (ISA) | ch. 11, 14E |

### 0.2 Atomics and memory ordering

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Weak memory models and the Linux memory model | `memory-barriers.txt` 3,016 lines; ordering vocabulary matches 5,596 code lines in 855 files; the Alpha dependent-load barrier was folded into `READ_ONCE()` as of v4.15 | Zijlstra, McKenney, Rutland, Alpha maintainers | see left | A sequentially consistent model | **AVOIDED**: sequentially consistent, in order (7.2.1†, 7.2.4†) | ch. 6, 14B, 14F |
| 2 | LL/SC versus single-instruction atomics; failing compare-and-swap loops | arm64 `lockref` thread (74 messages); Marvell "~25x retries for one cmpxchg" | Torvalds, Marvell engineers | "if you have a case of failing cmpxchg a lot, your cache coherency is simply bad" | A load-for-ownership hint | **OPEN** (suggestion N7) | 14E, HV 10 |
| 3 | Mixed-size atomics and the bit-operation API | David Laight: code uses bit functions on `int[]`; POPL 2017 paper not read | Laight, Lutomirski | not measured | A single atomic width rule | **OPEN** | 14D |

### 0.3 Early hardware bugs

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A user instruction that stops the CPU (F00F), Cyrix coma, FDIV | `intel.c`: "lets nonprivileged users lock up the system"; `cyrix.c` `X86_BUG_COMA`; `fpu/bugs.c` boot-time FDIV test (dates are not in the files) | x86 maintainers | not measured | An instruction-set test of every encoding from user mode (N10) | **OPEN**: suggested rule N10 | 14F, HV 11 |
| 2 | Hidden firmware mode (SMM) and firmware-set MTRRs | `usb-legacy-support.rst` (Pavlik, 2004): "the SMM BIOS isn't expecting the CPU to be in 64-bit mode" | Pavlik, Rodriguez | system crashes (qualitative) | No hidden mode (N11) | **AVOIDED** by design: no SMM-like mode in the manual (inferred from its absence) | 14F |
| 3 | Errata as a phenomenon | 110 Kconfig options named ERRATUM/ERRATA under `arch/` (64 arm64, 32 arm, 14 riscv); 45 x86 bug flags; 58 rows of an x86 vulnerability blacklist | all | `bugs.c` alone is 3,830 lines | Fewer features to be wrong about; a way to identify the chip (P4) | **MITIGATED** (no silicon yet) | ch. 1, 13 |

### 0.4 Interrupts and timers

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Interrupt controller programming and firmware lying about routing | `io_apic.c`: the SiS read-modify-write bug, mptables that "lie about the timer IRQ"; x2APIC enabled by firmware panics a kernel that does not support it (Liu, 7 Jan 2015) | x86 interrupt maintainers | boot panic with a message | Message-signalled, per-device interrupts; a defined handoff state | **OPEN**: controller and routing are decision R1 (7.8.3†) | ch. 10, 14B, 14F, A.5 159 |
| 2 | Shared level lines, "nobody cared", storms | `spurious.c`: a line is killed after 100,000 interrupts if more than 99,900 are unhandled; 4.3BSD `zvcnt_max = 5000 /* in 8 sec */`; Intel PCH "boot interrupts" killed shared lines | Heo (2010 comment), Kelley (Intel) | a dead device | One interrupt source per device | **OPEN** (R7 device error reporting) | 14B, 14F, A.5 157-158 |
| 3 | The TSC cannot be trusted | Gleixner: "the stupid hardware is not reliable whether it has some 'I claim to be reliable tag' on it or not"; "500+ different variants of borked timers" | Gleixner | not measured | One architectural counter that is monotonic and constant | **MITIGATED**: a 64-bit free-running counter and a compare register (5.5.4†); no user-mode read yet (proposal P3, appendix B.1.2) | ch. 10, 14B |
| 4 | Ordering of data and interrupt behind a bridge | `msi-howto.rst`: with pin interrupts "the interrupt may arrive before all the data has arrived in memory"; a handler must read a device register | PCI maintainers | an extra register read per interrupt | Interrupt as a write on the same ordered path | **MITIGATED, with a gap**: the device writes the result and status word and only then raises the interrupt (2.4.3†), and the handler reads the `IRQ` register (5.7.1); the manual does not say that the bus keeps the interrupt behind the posted DMA write, so the guarantee is the device's order, not the bus's | A.5 160-161 |
| 5 | Timer tick versus tickless; timer wheel rework | dynticks off-case must not slow; timer wheel reworked four times in June-July 2016 | Weisbecker, Gleixner | not measured | A per-event compare register | **MITIGATED**: a programmable compare register (5.5.4†) | 14B |
| 6 | The clock watchdog and its reference | 2026 rewrite: "To address this and bring back sanity to the watchdog, rewrite the code completely"; Gleixner: "BIOSes are insane by definition"; the TSC marked unstable on an SGI machine by a skewed RTC | Gleixner, Wiesner | `tsc skewed 703664 ns ... over watchdog 'sgi_rtc'` | A counter that does not need a second counter to trust it | **OPEN**: one counter, no second reference clock (5.5.4†) | 14H, A.5 188 |
| 7 | Fixed-address entry points (vsyscall) | `vsyscall_64.c` (2014): "It interferes with ASLR" | Lutomirski | not measured | A vDSO-like page at a random address | **AVOIDED**: no such page | 14F |

### 0.5 Bus design

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Devices that cannot reach all of memory: ISA 16 MB, DMA masks, bounce buffers | ISA DMA reaches the "lowest 16 MB of physical memory" and may not cross 64/128 KB; zones and masks grew from it | DMA-API maintainers | Stevens (Chromium, 2021): with bounce buffers, fio on a passed-through NVMe drive under virtio-iommu rose "2.8x, 4.7x, 3.6x, and 3.6x" for 4k to 128k blocks | Full-width device addressing plus an I/O translation unit | **OPEN**: devices use 40-bit physical addresses until an address-translation unit exists (5.4.2†) | 14B, 14F, A.5 153 |
| 2 | Devices as DMA masters: protection | Thunderbolt/PCIe devices read memory without OS knowledge; Thunderclap (NDSS 2019) shows an IOMMU alone is not enough | Markettos et al.; Intel/Linux Thunderbolt developers | IOMMU bypass "within seconds of connecting" (Markettos et al. 2019) | Translation on by default; no kernel pointers in descriptors (N8) | **OPEN** (R6; a user-space driver is as trusted as its device, 5.8.2†) | 14B, 14F, HV 11 |
| 3 | PCI configuration, enumeration and resource assignment | about 30 `pci=` boot options (BIOS access, config mechanism, MMCONFIG, bus numbers, BAR/ROM assignment, MPS policy, PIRQ table); hot-plug windows default to 256 B I/O and 2 MB MMIO | PCI maintainers | each option is a platform that failed | Identification register per slot, resources fixed by the platform | **MITIGATED**: bus as a device, each slot found by its `ID` register (2.6, 5.3.1†) | 14F, A.5 155-156 |
| 4 | Cache coherence for DMA and weakly ordered MMIO | `dma_sync_*`, bounce buffers, "safe register" reads; `cachetlb` hooks | DMA, ARM, MIPS, PowerPC maintainers | Hellwig: cache maintenance stubbed unless needed (2018) | Coherent DMA | **MITIGATED**: DMA buffers mapped uncached (5.4.1†) with `FENCE` at the doorbell (2.2.2†, 5.1.4†); cost: CPU reads of a DMA buffer go to the bus | ch. 7, 14B, A.5 161 |
| 5 | Device errors: removed or failed devices, spinning drivers | `pci-error-recovery.rst`: reporting lets drivers "avoid deadlocking in spinloops, waiting for some i/o-space register to change, when it never will" | Vepstas, Lary, Mason | not measured | A bus error that is an error return | **MITIGATED**: a read of non-existent memory is a precise `BUSFAULT` abort (2.3.1†); a timed-out posted write is a machine check (2.3.2†) | A.5 162 |
| 6 | Vendor quirks: NVMe/AHCI, USB host controllers, ARM board files | NVMe device "claiming 1.3 compliance" lacking a feature (Hellwig, 29 Jul 2020); four USB host controller generations with companion controllers; ARM board churn ("a f*cking pain in the ass", Torvalds, 17 Mar 2011) | Hellwig, Feng, Torvalds | quirk per model | Versioned, compliance-tested identification; board as data | **MITIGATED**: slots with a common register layout (5.2, 5.5.1†, 5.5.4†) | 14F, A.5 164-166 |
| 7 | Mapping-register buses (Unibus): scarce map registers and bus resets | 4.3BSD `uba.c`: `UBA_NEED16` limits to the first 128 map registers; BDP waiters sleep; `ubareset` calls every driver's reset | VAX-era kernel developers | not measured | Plentiful address translation | **OPEN** (R6) | 14, A.5 168 |

### 0.6 Graphics and display

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 (chapter 3) | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Closed GPU firmware and drivers | Torvalds, the week before 19 Jun 2012 (reported by Ars): NVIDIA "the single worst company"; Nouveau reclocking depends on vendor firmware ("Managed by firmware") | Torvalds, Nouveau, Collabora (Panfrost: months of reverse engineering, no hardware) | years of reverse engineering | Documented register interfaces | **NOT APPLICABLE** unless a PCI Express card is added (3.5.2†) | 14F, A.5 172-177 |
| 2 | Mode setting in user space, then in the kernel | KMS had been in the mainline "a year" in Jan 2010 (Corbet); Skeggs proposed dropping UMS then; legacy infrastructure removed in a 14-patch series, 1 Dec 2023 (about 14 years later) | Skeggs, Airlie, Zimmermann | Airlie: no kernel "that works on all Intel chipsets without backporting patches" (2010) | One kernel mode-setting model from the start | **MITIGATED**: the driver sets mode and base address (3.3.2†) | 14F, A.5 169, 171 |
| 3 | Tearing, vblank and page flip | Packard 2013: both vblank-blit methods have costs (half frame rate, or late blit artifacts); cursor and planes not synchronised until atomic mode setting (Vetter 2015) | Packard, Vetter | see left | Scan-out base register latched at vblank; blitter start-at-vblank | **OPEN**: BLT64 (3.1.4†) lacks start-at-vblank; mode and base must latch together | 14F, A.5 181-182 |
| 4 | Scan-out FIFO underrun | i915 underrun detection reworked (Syrjälä, Vetter, May 2014) | Intel graphics developers | corrupted display | Underrun status, fixed bus priority for the video master | **OPEN**: the line buffer in 3.4.2† has no underrun rule | A.5 183 |
| 5 | GPU hangs and resets, indefinite fences | `drm-uapi.rst`: resets cannot be disabled "without tainting the kernel"; `dma-buf.rst`: deadlocks the kernel cannot prevent | DRM developers | not measured | Timeouts in the device | **OPEN**: BLT64 needs reset and error status | A.5 174-175 |
| 6 | EDID and the HDMI transmitter | `edid.rst`: bad EDID makes "the computer ... unusable"; ADV7511 has four register maps on four I2C addresses; HPD status survives suspend (Das, 22 Apr 2026) | Pinchart, Das | qualitative | Fallback mode; transmitter state owned by the video device | **OPEN**: 3.3.1† says "set up once"; real chips need more | A.5 184-185 |
| 7 | DisplayPort link training fallback | 5 Oct 2026: fallback "dead code on every Type-C board"; Intel's fallback series reached five revisions | Bobier, Deak | see left | Transmitter chip with tested fallback | **MITIGATED** by chip (3.3.1†) | 14H, A.5 192 |
| 8 | HDMI licensing | HDMI Forum rejected an open HDMI 2.1 driver (Deucher, Feb 2024); first FRL patches May 2026 (heise) | Deucher | n/a | Protocol in a closed chip | **MITIGATED** by putting HDMI in the transmitter chip (3.3.1†) | A.5 186 |
| 9 | Device-specific 2D acceleration | Packard 2014: software drew text at 9.3 million glyphs/s vs 3.2 million for GL points; Glamor to replace per-driver 2D | Packard, Anholt | see left | Copy/fill engines only | **MITIGATED**: BLT64 for copies and fills | A.5 179 |
| 10 | Network-transparent display protocols | Sharp 2004: replies cost round trips; NeWS "not economically or politically viable" (Handbook) | Sharp, Packard, the Handbook authors | latency | Local devices with a ring | **AVOIDED** by local devices (3.1†) | 14F, 14G, A.5 187 |
| 11 | GPU memory leakage and Rowhammer | LeftoverLocals (16 Jan 2024): ~5.5 MB per invocation on an RX 7900 XT; GPUHammer (arXiv 2507.08166) | Sorensen, Trail of Bits, Lin/Qu/Saileshwar | see left | Clear or tag device memory (N9) | **OPEN** (N9) | 14F, A.5 180 |
| 12 | Terminal and host split by a slow serial link | Blit, 1981-84 (Pike 1983): "The greatest problem, though, is certainly the low bandwidth"; the editor had to be written in two parts, and "writing one program in two pieces is much harder than writing two programs"; Plan 9 moved the window system into a file server (8½, 1991) | Pike, Locanthi, Reiser | split programs; protocol with CRC and acknowledgements | A terminal on the host's bus, no link | **AVOIDED**: the terminal is devices on the bus (3.1); the Blit had no blit engine, BLT64 goes beyond it (14I.7) | 14I, A.6 197-204 |

### 0.7 Security and speculation

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Speculation leaks (Spectre, Meltdown, MDS, Foreshadow, Retbleed, Downfall) | 2018 onward; "at least nine years" of exposure for Gather (Downfall); the first MDS fix was incomplete (RIDL authors) | Gleixner, Woodhouse, Torvalds, Kleen, discoverers | Retbleed fix: 68 files, 1,783 new lines, 14% to 39% overhead; Gather mitigation "up to 50%" for some loads | Permission checks before data forwarding; clear architectural statements of what speculation may leak | **AVOIDED today** (in order, no speculation); **OPEN** for a future out-of-order core (7.11, HV rules C1-C16, N1-N11) | ch. 4, HV, 14D, 14E |
| 2 | Row-level DRAM disturbance | Rowhammer: unprivileged `CLFLUSH` made it practical; DDR4 TRRespass "12 of the 42 DIMMs" | Project Zero, VUSec | see left | Privileged cache flush; refresh-management command (N4) | **OPEN** | HV 3.5, 10 |
| 3 | Constant-time code undermined by hardware | Hertzbleed: frequency scaling makes timing data-dependent | Intel, discoverers | not measured | A constant-time mode bit in `PSW` (N6) | **OPEN** (N6) | HV 4 |
| 4 | Mitigation sprawl | 24 `MITIGATION_` options; maintainers refused knobs ("We are not intending to permit all the bullshit micro-management") | Woodhouse, Kleen | `bugs.c` 3,830 lines | Positive-enumeration of safety (N5) | **OPEN** | 14D, 14E |

### 0.8 Firmware and boot

| # | Pain | What, when, how long | Felt by | Cost | A different design would have | pdp64 | See |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Wrong firmware tables: a quirk per machine | ACPI quirks found by test-and-wait: "test result --> patch --> 1-2 weeks + result --> patch ... over and over" (Feng Tang, 9 Oct 2012); broken BIOSes (Delvare, Jan 2010) | Tang, Delvare, Dang | 1-2 week cycles | A single defined handoff state and memory-map format | **MITIGATED**: the boot design defines the reset state and handoff (4.2, 4.4); no UEFI (4.6) | 14B, `boot.md` |
| 2 | Starting the other cores | INIT/SIPI/SIPI with a poll of up to 10 s; 305-405 µs per core on a 4-core desktop (Len Brown, 16 Aug 2015) | Len Brown | see left | An event-based release | **MITIGATED**: a write to a per-core start register releases a core (7.8.1†) | 14B |
| 3 | Firmware blobs and signed firmware | `fw_update(8)` ships non-free firmware separately; Nouveau needs vendor firmware; Linux firmware ABI guidelines | OpenBSD, Nouveau | n/a | Hardware that needs no firmware | **MITIGATED**: the HDMI transmitter needs none (3.3.1†) | A.5 173 |

**What the overview says in one paragraph.** The pains that lasted longest are the ones where the hardware gave the kernel a state it could not describe or restart (interrupted system calls, NMI nesting), a boundary it could not defend cheaply (the kernel in every address space), or a bus on which devices and firmware each followed their own rules (PCI quirks, ACPI tables, DMA masks). pdp64 avoids the first by precise, restartable faults (9.2.6†, 6.8†), avoids the third by making the bus a set of identified slots (2.6), and leaves the second **open** for a future out-of-order core. The open items that the round-5 reading adds are the display ones: a latched base register, start-at-vblank, an underrun policy, a transmitter that is more than "a few registers", and a stated rule for a hung or failed device (R6, R7).

## 1. The short version

Read the current Linux tree for what hurts and the picture is consistent. **Three costs dominate**, and pdp64's design touches each in a different way.

1. **The cost of crossing the privilege boundary and of the state kept across it.** Linux's x86-64 entry code is a long list of defences: a page-table switch (a `mov` to `cr3` "on the order of a hundred cycles" at every entry and exit, in the developers' words, `Documentation/arch/x86/pti.rst`, [G]), a `swapgs` that "must nest perfectly" (`Documentation/arch/x86/entry_64.rst`, [G]), register clearing, speculation barriers. pdp64's entry writes no memory and has no speculation, so it escapes most of this today (estimated 4 to 5 instructions against 45 to 70, survey section 8, a paper estimate). **The exposure is the future out-of-order core and the single page-table base**: the whole defence exists because the kernel is mapped in every user address space, which is exactly what pdp64 does (system design D6).
2. **Everything caused by hardware that reorders, caches and speculates.** Barriers, memory-ordering documentation, cache-maintenance hooks, DMA bounce buffers, RCU, per-architecture TLB flush rules. In the current Linux tree the vocabulary of ordering (`smp_mb`, `READ_ONCE`, and relatives) matches **5,596 lines of code in 855 files** and 743 lines of documentation; `Documentation/memory-barriers.txt` is 3,016 lines [P, counted]. pdp64 is sequentially consistent, in-order and cache-coherent between CPUs, so almost all of it **does not arise** (ISA 17.1); the cost is moved onto the implementer of any future fast core (readiness review, section 6.10).
3. **Everything caused by hardware that is defective or inconsistent between vendors.** 110 Kconfig options named *ERRATUM* or *ERRATA* under `arch/` (64 arm64, 32 arm, 14 riscv) [P, counted]; 45 x86 bug flags and 58 rows of an x86 vulnerability blacklist; 19 files and 4,164 lines of `hw-vuln` documentation; a 3,830-line `bugs.c` that only chooses mitigations. No design avoids this for a real chip. What a design can do is to have fewer features to be wrong about, and a way to learn what the chip is (P4), a recovery path that is short (principle b), and honesty that this is not yet tested: **pdp64 has no silicon, so it has no errata, which is not the same as having none.**

**Where pdp64 is exposed** (detail in section 15): software-managed accessed and dirty bits (an extra fault on first touch of every page, Q2); one page-table base for both halves with no speculation defence if the core ever speculates; broadcast `TLBI` per page with no range or by-ASID form until P5; machine checks that halt instead of recovering; a 39-bit address space; no defined cross-modifying-code rule; interrupt controller, IPIs, DMA protection and virtualization not yet defined (R1, R2, R6, R8); cache aliasing and non-coherent DMA not yet faced.

## 2. How much of a kernel exists to work around hardware: the counts

The unit of evidence is *how much of the current kernel exists to work around this*. Two kinds of measure, both **[P]** (practice: the code itself) and both described exactly in Appendix B.

**Structural measures**: a named file, table or option set, counted with one command each. The most telling are in this table.

| Measure | Tree | Value | Unit |
|---|---|---:|---|
| Kconfig options named *ERRATUM/ERRATA anywhere under arch | linux | 110 | options |
| Kconfig options named *ERRATUM/ERRATA in arch/arm64/Kconfig | linux | 64 | options |

| CPU model rows in the vulnerability blacklist (VULNBL_*) | linux | 58 | rows |
| MITIGATION_* Kconfig options in arch/x86/Kconfig | linux | 24 | options |
| files in Documentation/admin-guide/hw-vuln | linux | 19 | files |

| files under arch mentioning flush_dcache_page, flush_cache_, flush_icache_ | linux | 131 | files |
