* **Distinct fetched documents or threads with a quoted statement: 29** (the other 24 fetches are indexes, searches, listings, failures or context). **Table rows above: 34**; rows combine several messages of one thread (the split-lock thread gives four rows, the `barrier_nospec` thread two, the `lockref` thread one, the RSB thread two). Plus one group of three documents read in the local tree. The number of separate quotations was **not counted one by one**; no figure for "statements" is claimed beyond the 34 rows. Round 4 did not add routine pages to raise the count.
* **Whole-thread reading.** For four lore threads (RSB 20 messages, split lock 145, `lockref` 74, `barrier_nospec` 54) the saved text holds every message, but I read the messages of the people named in the rows, not all 293; the rows say so.

### Not done in round 4

* **Not reached:** the KAISER paper itself (the PDF gave no text through the browser: `corpus4/doc0002.txt` is 48 bytes), the Rowhammer paper by Kim et al. (PDF only), the GoFetch paper, the Hertzbleed paper (project pages were read), the POPL 2017 mixed-size paper cited by Zijlstra, Theo's and Bluhm's slides (PDF only), the per-process KPTI discussion of Dec 2017, the vDSO/TSC and MSI/GIC posts (one DuckDuckGo query for TSC returned a single unrelated boot report; a lore search for Linus on AVX-512 found nothing), NPTL and BKL slices of 2002 and 2003, FreeBSD months before 2018 (the `freebsd-arch` 2017-December thread index was read; a keyword search of it found no PCID, TLB or speculation subject), the NetBSD `port-vax` months of the 1990s (port-vax dates to 1994; the one 2012 month read held two spam messages; choosing months without a search engine is blind), `tech-smp` 2007.
* **Unverified or read in part:** the Linus 2009 NVMHCI post (the question it answers was not in the saved text, so it is **not** used above); the GoFetch FAQ answers; the `port-vax` thread of Nov 2023 (`ptelen fault in system space`), seen only as a search snippet and **not used**.

## 14F. Round 5: coverage check, bus design, GPU and display

Round 5 (23 Oct 2026, from about 11:20 local; the author removed the time limit during the round) was the last round. Its purpose was to find what the first four rounds had **missed**, not to add more of the same. Method: (1) a coverage matrix, [`codegen-data/kernelpain/coverage-matrix.tsv`](codegen-data/kernelpain/coverage-matrix.tsv), built by keyword counts over the three documents followed by reading the lines that matched (a keyword hit is not coverage: many hits are the word in a vocabulary list); (2) for every topic marked missing or thin, the local kernel trees first (label **[D-local]**: `~/claude/kernel-src/linux/Documentation`, `kernel/`, `arch/`, and the 4.3BSD tree in `~/claude/facetlang/unixtree`), then the web (label **[D]** developer statement, **[P]** paper or project page, **[S]** secondary press report). The author added three requests during the round: a GPU and graphics group, old graphics pain (tearing, double buffering, underruns) and HDMI / display link pain, and the Blit lineage of remote display. Chapter 3 of the manual is [`manual/03-graphics.md`](manual/03-graphics.md); each finding below says which of its decisions it supports or challenges.

**Important limit of the local trees.** The Linux copy under `~/claude/kernel-src/linux` has `Documentation/`, `arch/`, `kernel/`, `mm/`, `include/` but **no `drivers/` except `iommu` and `irqchip`**, and the BSD copies have only `sys/{kern,vm,uvm,...}`. Pages of `Documentation/gpu/` that are only `kernel-doc` references to driver files are therefore empty here; I read only text that is actually in the files. The `adv7511` driver source is not in the tree; its device-tree binding is.

**Pace, honestly.** One browser, one fetch at a time, `politewait.py` before every fetch (20 s; 30 s for lwn.net). I wrote the fetcher `r5.py` (it calls `politewait.py` itself). Two fetches did not finish in the page-load window (Phoronix, RealWorldTech) and docslib's Blit page came back with 137 bytes and are listed as failed. After the RealWorldTech request the browser's driver timed out (`Read timed out`, 120 s); the browser family was at about 1.45 GB, so I stopped it with `browsectl quit` (clean shutdown, one pending search directive cleared by the quit) and started it again. No 429, 503, captcha or block page was seen at any host. lwn.net was requested directly (the ban was lifted): 8 pages at 30 s or more apart, no block. One honest slip: the helper `lk.py` still held a line that refused lwn.net URLs; I deleted that line (the author lifted the ban). Another: the first Ars Technica fetch was saved as the previous search page because my script took the "newest file" before the browser had written the new one; I repaired the fetcher to wait for a new file and re-read the page (`doc0004` in the index is that bad copy and is marked failed).

### Coverage summary

The matrix has **95 topics** in nine groups. Before round 5 (my reading of the three documents): **13 covered well, 25 thin, 57 missing**. After round 5: **52 covered well, 40 thin, 3 missing**. "Covered well after" means there is now at least one primary text (or, for local-tree evidence, the file text itself) in 14F or Appendix A.5 and the topic has a stated pdp64 consequence; it does not mean the topic is exhausted. Per group, before to after (covered well / thin / missing):

| Group | Topics | Before | After |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Memory system and CPUs | 22 | 7 / 9 / 6 | 11 / 11 / 0 |
| Early hardware bugs | 5 | 0 / 3 / 2 | 0 / 5 / 0 |
| Interrupts and timers | 10 | 2 / 5 / 3 | 8 / 2 / 0 |
| Bus design | 15 | 4 / 6 / 5 | 9 / 6 / 0 |
| GPU and graphics | 16 | 0 / 1 / 15 | 7 / 9 / 0 |
| Old graphics pain | 9 | 0 / 0 / 9 | 5 / 3 / 1 |
| HDMI and display link | 10 | 0 / 0 / 10 | 5 / 3 / 2 |
| Remote display lineage | 2 | 0 / 0 / 2 | 1 / 1 / 0 |
| Additions found while reading | 6 | 0 / 1 / 5 | 6 / 0 / 0 |

**Still missing after round 5 (3):** racing the beam in driver developers' writing, HDMI audio, DisplayPort MST and DSC. For these **no primary record was read** (not "no record exists"). Where the matrix says thin the reason is in its column "what was done".

**Round 6 update (matrix now 97 topics; read the last two sentences for the final counts).** The row 'The Blit and NeWS lineage' was split into three: 'The Blit: hardware, software, protocol, trade-offs' (thin to **covered well**, 14I.1-I.3), 'NeWS and X11 as successors of the Blit' (stays **thin**: no source read makes the claim) and a new row 'Plan 9 window system: 8½, rio, `/dev/bitblt`, `/dev/draw`' (**covered well**, 14I.5). Two thin rows were then worked in 14K (level versus edge triggering; BSD graphics). Counts: before round 6 **52 covered well, 40 thin, 3 missing** (of 95); after round 6 **56 covered well, 38 thin, 3 missing** (of 97). The remote-display group is now 3 covered well, 1 thin.

| The big kernel lock stayed on the to-do list for most of its life | "The removal of the big kernel lock (BKL) has been on the kernel community's 'to do' list almost since that lock was first added to make the kernel work on multiprocessor systems."; "the BKL can still cause unwanted latencies at times"; "hundreds of BKL call sites remained in the kernel"; "Some of this work was motivated by the realtime tree" | Jonathan Corbet, `The real BKL end game`, LWN, 26 Jan 2011 | LWN 424657 (`doc0019`) | [D] | ch. 13 / R3: a single-lock first SMP is cheap to build and expensive to leave; the manual's SMP chapter should not rely on a global lock surviving past the first board |
| Reverse mapping replaced the 2.4 VM approach and then needed its own lock scheme | "The initial implementation used a global lock, which, of course, did not perform particularly well."; the finer lock "is implemented with bit operations ... the required atomic operations can be expensive"; Linus asked that rmap not create another hashed spinlock; objrmap "can be vastly slower than the rmap scheme it replaces" | Linux Kernel Developers Summit 2003 notes (unsigned in the saved text), Rik van Riel's talk | USENIX page (`doc0059`) | [D] (notes) | ch. 5: a page-to-mappings reverse index is a software structure; the PTE has no room for it. The 2.4 replacement of Rik's VM is known to me only from a search snippet of an LWN page of 24 Jan 2002 and is **not** used |
| Highmem is the least popular feature | "High memory is one of the least popular features of the Linux kernel. | Highmem as the kernel documents it | "High memory (highmem) is used when the size of physical memory approaches or exceeds the maximum size of virtual memory. At that point it becomes impossible for the kernel to keep all of the available physical memory mapped at all times." | Peter Zijlstra, `Documentation/mm/highmem.rst` | local tree | [D-local] | same |
| Kernel stacks are a weak point | "The kernel stack in Linux is arguably a weak point in the system's design: it is small enough that kernel developers must constantly be aware of what they put on the stack to avoid overflows."; "a stack that grows too large simply overwrites whatever memory is located just below the allocated range"; "This sort of surprise led to the expansion of the x86-64 kernel stack to 16KB for the 3.15 release" | Corbet, `Virtually mapped kernel stacks`, LWN, 22 Jun 2016 | LWN 692208 (`doc0017`) | [D] | ch. 8, 9: the pdp64 kernel stack size and a guard page below it should be a stated rule; pdp64 pages are small enough that a guard page is cheap |
| The fix has a price in time and in DMA | virtually mapped stacks make `clone()` "take about 1.5µs longer", Linus: "that problem needs to be fixed before this should be merged"; "Memory mapped into the vmalloc range ... cannot easily be used for direct memory access (DMA) I/O ... there is some code in the kernel that does it anyway" | Corbet, 22 Jun 2016 (reporting Andy Lutomirski's patch set) | LWN 692208 | [D] | ch. 7: DMA from a stack is the same bug as DMA from a non-contiguous buffer when no I/O translation exists (R6) |
| `VMAP_STACK` today | "Enable this if you want the use virtually-mapped kernel stacks with guard pages. This causes kernel stack overflows to be caught immediately rather than causing difficult-to-diagnose corruption." | `arch/Kconfig` help text | local tree | [D-local] | same |
| NMI nesting: IRET unmasks the NMI | "an NMI is considered complete when an iret instruction is executed."; "If the NMI handler triggers either a page fault or breakpoint, the iret used to return from those exceptions will re-enable NMIs."; "on x86_64 ... the stack pointer is set to a fixed address (per CPU). If another NMI comes in before the first NMI handler is complete, the new NMI will write all over the preempted NMIs stack."; "NMI handlers must neither trigger a page fault nor hit a breakpoint ... this restriction prevents NMI handlers from using memory allocated by `vmalloc()`" | Steven Rostedt, `The x86 NMI iret problem`, LWN, 7 Mar 2012 | LWN 484932 (`doc0016`) | [D] | ISA 7, E40: pdp64 priority 7 on entry; the rule to write down is that **the return instruction must not be what ends the masking of a non-maskable event**, and that a handler may fault |
| The kernel's own description of the same | "NMIs can nest if NMIs get unmasked due an IRET during NMI processing" and the comment then lists three states (not running, executing, latched); "the latch is binary, thus multiple NMIs triggering, when one is running, are ignored. Only one NMI is restarted." | `arch/x86/kernel/nmi.c` comment | local tree | [D-local] | same |
| `espfix`: IRET to a 16-bit segment loses half the stack pointer | "The IRET instruction, when returning to a 16-bit segment, only restores the bottom 16 bits of the user space stack pointer. | Hot-plug windows are guesses | `hpiosize` "The fixed amount of bus space which is reserved for hotplug bridge's IO window. | Boot interrupts: INTx forwarded to a legacy controller | "When in-band legacy INTx messages are forwarded to the PCH, they in turn trigger a new interrupt for which the OS likely lacks a handler. This disabled IRQ now prevents valid usage by an existing interrupt which may happen to share the IRQ line" | Sean V. Kelley (Intel), `Documentation/PCI/boot-interrupts.rst` | local tree | [D-local] | ch. 10, R1: a shared level line is the hazard; one interrupt source per device (message or per-slot line) avoids the shared-line kill |

Boot with apic=debug and send a ...")" | `arch/x86/kernel/apic/io_apic.c` | local tree | [D-local] | ch. 10, R1: an interrupt controller programmed through an index register and a data register is read-modify-write fragile; a timer that fails to arrive is a boot-time panic |

Theodore Markettos, Colin Rothwell, Brett Gutstein, Allison Pearce, Peter Neumann, Simon Moore, Robert Watson, *Thunderclap*, NDSS, Feb 2019 | the paper itself, `ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/ndss2019_05A-1_Markettos_paper.pdf` (read in round 6 by the PDF method of 14I; round 5 had used `thunderclap.io`, whose current text has been altered, see 14J) | [P] | R6: the translation unit must be on by default, the descriptors a device reads must not hold kernel pointers, and the unit of protection (a 4 KB page) is larger than a descriptor or a buffer, so a buffer must not share a page with anything the device may not read |

| Two memory managers | "TTM was the first DRM memory manager to be developed and tried to be a one-size-fits-them all solution ... This resulted in a large, complex piece of code that turned out to be hard to use for driver development. GEM started as an Intel-sponsored project in reaction to TTM's complexity." | `Documentation/gpu/drm-mm.rst` | local tree | [D-local] | 3.4.2: one framebuffer in main memory is the UMA case, which is the simple case |
| GEM's own announcement | "We've spent the last couple of weeks writing a different manager for the kernel, called 'gem' ... It takes the lessons we've learned from TTM and constructs just the API we need"; "handle the essentially NUMA environment unique to modern graphics hardware"; "we can no longer insist that each application save a complete copy of their graphics state so that the card can be re-initialized from user space at each context switch"; the biggest missing feature "is proper latency-throttling where we'd like to keep the ring nearly empty and pend new requests while the ring executes older ones"; "Cache synchronization is handled automatically by the kernel"; `pwrite` "eliminates most cache effects from clflush as pwrite uses non-temporal stores"; 64-bit sizes and pointers in every ioctl to avoid compat translation | Keith Packard (with Eric Anholt), to Dave Airlie, 13 May 2008 (posted on LWN 27 May 2008) | LWN 283798 (`doc0026`) | [D] | 3.1.4, 3.4.1: a **ring kept nearly empty** to bound latency is the descriptor-ring discipline the manual already wants; uncached framebuffer writes and non-temporal stores are two answers to the same clflush cost |

So far I don't think there is one kernel release (even stable) that works on all Intel chipsets without backporting patches."; Corbet: "The main objection to the removal of this code is that BSD-based systems do not support KMS" | Corbet, `The end of user-space mode setting?`, LWN, 13 Jan 2010 | LWN 369706 (`doc0008`) | [D] | 3.1: mode setting in the kernel (a driver that writes the display registers) is the only model the manual has; the 2010 pain was moving **out of** user space |

### Old graphics pain: tearing, buffering, vblank, underruns

| Pain | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tearing and the two ways to avoid it | "In current drivers, there are essentially two different ways to make this happen: Insert a command into the ring which blocks execution until a suitable time immediately preceding the blt operation. Queue the blt operation at vblank time so that it executes before the scanout starts."; with the first, "if two vblank synchronized blts are queued at the same time, its possible for the second blt to be delayed by yet another frame, causing both applications to run at half of the frame rate"; with the second, "it can cause artifacts if the delay from the vblank event to the eventual execution of the blt command is too long" | Keith Packard, `Implementing Vblank Synchronization in the Present Extension`, 22 Jul 2013 | `keithp.com/blogs/present_sync/` (`doc0041`) | [D] | **Challenges 3.1.4**: BLT64 has go, busy and done-interrupt, but no way to start at the next vblank or once scan-out has passed a given line. Both of Packard's methods need one of those. A blit that races scan-out tears |
| Where the blit is queued from | three options: "Queue the blt from the interrupt handler; Queue the blt from a kernel thread running in response to the interrupt; Send an event up to user space"; "One can imagine having the desired command already present in the ring buffer and have the interrupt handler simply move the ring tail pointer value." | Packard, same | same | [D] | R2, 3.1.4: the descriptor ring with a doorbell is the structure Packard wishes for; the doorbell at vblank is the missing hook |
| Page flipping | "The kernel flipping API is asynchronous, and doesn't provide any abort mechanism."; "The applications buffer remains 'busy' until it is no longer being used for scanout"; "(The kernel DRM drivers use the term 'page flipping' to mean an atomic flip between one frame buffer and another, generally implemented by simply switching the address used for the scanout buffer.)" | Packard, same | same | [D] | **Supports** 3.4: two framebuffers and one base-address register is a flip; the register must latch at vblank, and the old buffer is busy until the next vblank |
| Display FIFO underrun | the i915 developers add "reliable" underrun detection: "I was looking at FIFO underruns a bit and came up with a few improvements to the FIFO underrun reporting code. Mainly this should lead to detecting FIFO a bit more more reliably on gmch platforms."; Vetter: "since we don't necessarily notice the underruns with the shared error interrupt"; "At least some of the reports we've seen suggest that modesets on the pch influence each another ..." | Ville Syrjälä, 16 May 2014; Daniel Vetter, 26 May 2014 | lore (`doc0038`, `doc0039`) | [D] | **Challenges 3.4.2**: a few-kilobyte line buffer fed from main memory will underrun when the bus is busy (the blitter, the processor's cache fills, the sound device). The manual needs an underrun status bit, a fixed priority for the video master, and a rule for what the screen does |
| The X server in the middle | "The X server doesn't have the information to decide which window should receive the event ... And even though X has handed responsibility for the final painting of the screen to the compositing manager, X still controls the front buffer and modesetting."; for a compositor that is always full screen the X step that "either copies the compositor back buffer to the front buffer or does a pageflip ... is another unnecessary context switch" | Wayland project documentation (author not given in the saved text) | `wayland.freedesktop.org/architecture.html` (`doc0032`) | [P] | 3.1: a local display owned by one program removes the middle man |
| Direct rendering | "With direct rendering, the client and the server share a video memory buffer. The client links to a rendering library ... that knows how to program the hardware and renders directly into the buffer." | same | same | [P] | 3.4: the pdp64 terminal is a direct-rendering design by construction |

### HDMI and display-link pain

| Pain | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wrong EDID makes the machine unusable | "Today, with the advent of Kernel Mode Setting, a graphics board is either correctly working because all components follow the standards - or the computer is unusable, because the screen remains dark after booting or it displays the wrong area. Cases when this happens are: The graphics board does not recognize the monitor. ... The monitor sends no or bogus EDID data. A KVM sends its own EDID data instead of querying the connected monitor."; `drm.edid_firmware`: "Broken monitors, graphic adapters, KVMs and EDIDless panels may send no or incorrect EDID data sets." | `Documentation/admin-guide/edid.rst`; `kernel-parameters.txt` | local tree | [D-local] | **Gap in 3.3**: the chapter has the driver set a mode but never says where the mode comes from. The EDID is read through the transmitter's I2C; the manual needs a fallback mode and an override, as Linux needed `edid_firmware` |
| The transmitter chip is four I2C devices | "The ADV7511, ADV7511W and ADV7513 are HDMI audio and video transmitters compatible with HDMI 1.4 and DVI 1.0. They support color space conversion, S/PDIF, CEC and HDCP. The transmitter input is parallel RGB or YUV data."; "The ADV7511/11W/13 internal registers are split into four pages exposed through different I2C addresses, creating four register maps" (`main`, `edid`, `cec`, `packet`); a power-down GPIO, four 1.8 V supplies and a 3.3 V supply, one interrupt | Laurent Pinchart (binding maintainer), `adi,adv7511.yaml` | local tree | [D-local] | **Challenges 3.3.1** ("set up once over an I2C-style control bus"): the control bus is four register maps, a hot-plug interrupt, a power-down line and several supplies; "a few registers" understates it |
| Hot-plug status survives suspend | "On RZ/G3E SMARC EVK, suspend-to-RAM via PSCI powers down the ADV7535 chip entirely, causing the HPD status bit to be in a stale state on resume ... the stale HPD IRQ is never cleared, leading to incorrect behaviour."; fix: "Clear the HPD status bit in adv7511_bridge_atomic_enable() after powering on, so that any HPD interrupt latched before or during power-loss is dismissed" | Biju Das (Renesas), `[PATCH v2] drm/bridge: adv7511: Clear HPD IRQ during atomic_enable()`, 22 Apr 2026 | lore (`doc0043`) | [D] | 3.3.1: the boot routine must clear the transmitter's latched status, not only write the mode |
| The register cache and the I2C bus | "adv7511_power_on() and adv7511_power_off() restore the register state using regcache_mark_dirty() and regcache_sync(), but nothing prevents other code paths from accessing the registers over I2C while the chip is powered down." (the patch author); Pinchart: "Please give a detailed example of a scenario where that can happen, and make sure you can reproduce the scenario and see the issue on your hardware." | phucduc.bui, RFC `use cache_only during power down`; Laurent Pinchart reply, 24 Jul 2026 | lore (`doc0044`); the lore search `doc0042` lists further 2026 patches "Stop power-on after cache sync failure" (28 Aug), "cancel HPD work during teardown" (28 Jul) (subjects only) | [D] | 3.3.1: the chip loses its registers when powered down; the pdp64 driver keeps the mode in the video device, not in the transmitter, or must re-send it on power-up |
| The HDMI Forum kept 2.1 out of open drivers | Deucher (AMD): "The HDMI Forum has rejected our proposal unfortunately. At this time an open source HDMI 2.1 implementation is not possible without running afoul of the HDMI Forum requirements."; in Feb 2023 he was "working with our [AMD] legal team to sort out what we can deliver while still complying with our obligations to HDMI Forum."; Ars: "Membership in the HDMI Forum is a minimum of $15,000." | Alex Deucher, Feb 2024, reported by Ars Technica | `arstechnica.com/gadgets/2024/02/...` (`doc0014`) | [S] | **Supports 3.3.1**: with the link protocol in a closed transmitter chip, the manual's CPU never has to publish an HDMI implementation |
| ... and then 2026 | "First patches are available for the Radeon kernel driver AMDGPU and the community driver Nouveau ... They support the Fixed Rate Link (FRL) introduced with HDMI 2.1" | Mark Mantel, heise online, 4 May 2026 | `heise.de/en/news/...` (`doc0015`; the saved text stops after the first paragraph at an advertisement marker) | [S] | the 2024 statement in the row above is therefore **not the current state**; read both. **Round 6, primary source:** Harry Wentland, "[PATCH v6 00/15] HDMI FRL and DSC Support for amdgpu", 20 May 2026 (lore, `20260520202929.555119-1-harry.wentland@amd.com`): "This patch series adds HDMI FRL and FRL DSC support to the amdgpu display driver."; "FRL will be disabled by default since we don't have the VRR bits yet."; the work "passed a representative subset of HDMI compliance"; and Fangzhi Zuo, "[PATCH v2] drm/amd/display: Enable HDMI FRL by default", 27 Aug 2026: FRL "was left disabled unless a user explicitly opted in via the amdgpu.dcfeaturemask module parameter". The cover letter does not mention the HDMI Forum, so whether the Forum's objection was lifted is **not stated** in any source read |

### Remote display lineage (the Blit, X11, NeWS)

| Pain | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Round-trip latency is the cost of network transparency | "Since X has a network-transparent protocol, it may be run on high-latency connections such as dial-up or DSL Internet links, and on these links replies take long enough to arrive that the delay may have a noticeable effect on application performance. In analyzing this aspect of X performance, we speak of round-trip latency, and look for techniques to avoid or hide it"; "For more than 15 years, new features have been added to Xlib by accretion, rather than with careful design."; at start-up "five round-trips to the server" | Jamey Sharp, *How Xlib is Implemented (And What We're Doing About It)*, USENIX FREENIX 2004 | `usenix.org/events/usenix04/...` (`doc0034`) | [P] | A display driven by a protocol pays a round trip per query; a display of local devices with a descriptor ring pays none: **supports** the manual's choice |
| Fast local paths grew beside the protocol | direct rendering (Wayland page above); DRI3 and Present replaced DRI2 with application-allocated buffers and file descriptors (Packard's status line: "At this point, the DRI3 extension is complete and the Present extension is largely complete") | Packard, 22 Jul 2013 | `doc0041` | [D] | same |
| The Blit and NeWS | the Blit paper's abstract (Rob Pike, "sometime in 1983", cat-v.org page; the paper itself was **not read in round 5**; it was read in round 6, see 14I): "The Blit is a programmable bitmap graphics terminal designed specifically to run with the Unix operating system. The software in the terminal provides an asynchronous multi-window environment, and thereby exploits the multiprogramming capabilities of the Unix system which have been largely under-utilized because of the restrictions of conventional terminals."; the page also offers a 1982 demonstration by Pike and Bart Locanthi. A NeWS co-author's account of why NeWS lost is in 14G (Rosenthal, 2024: licensing). **Round 5 note, superseded by 14I:** the abstract does not say the Blit was an m68k receiving code and pixels over a serial link; the full papers (14I.7) confirm the 68000, the serial link and the downloaded code, contradict "pixels" and "bit-block-transfer engine", and do not mention X11 or NeWS as successors | Rob Pike, 1983 (abstract) | `doc.cat-v.org/bell_labs/blit/` (`doc0072`) | [P], abstract only | chapter 3 (3.1.1): see 14I.7 for the verdict by part (corrected in round 6) |

### What round 5 says about chapter 3, decision by decision

| Chapter 3 decision | Round 5 finding | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 3.1.2 framebuffer in the address space, scanned out through a line buffer | Vetter / Packard / Syrjälä: tearing, buffer-busy and underruns are the cost of a scan-out engine reading memory that others write | **Supports**, with three additions the chapter lacks: a vblank-latched base register, an underrun status, a fixed bus priority for the video master |
| 3.1.4 BLT64 with go, busy and done interrupt | Packard's two queueing methods; GEM's "ring nearly empty"; reset and error propagation in `drm-uapi.rst`; software beating the GPU on glyphs (Glamor) | **Supports** the device; **challenges** the register list: it needs a way to start at vblank or after a given scan line, a reset bit and an error status. A blitter is worth it for copies and fills, not for text |
| 3.3.1 HDMI via a transmitter chip configured over I2C | ADV7511 binding (four register maps, interrupt, power-down); Das (stale HPD after suspend); regcache patches; HDMI Forum rejection of open 2.1 | **Supports** keeping the protocol in the chip; **challenges** "a few registers": the boot routine must read the EDID, handle hot-plug and clear latched status |
| 3.3.2 registers set the mode and the base address | Vetter: the old non-atomic interface let planes and modes change out of step and reach unsupported intermediate states | **Challenges** unless the update is latched together |
| 3.4.1 framebuffer uncached, FENCE before go | GEM post (clflush cost, non-temporal stores, kernel-managed cache synchronisation); `pat.rst` (WC and UC aliasing needs a rule) | **Supports** the uncached choice; the cost is paid per pixel by the CPU, so drawing belongs to the blitter |
| 3.4.2 framebuffer in main memory, a few-KB line buffer | underrun posts; `hmm.rst` on bus bandwidth | **Challenges** the line-buffer size without an underrun policy |
| 3.5 a standard PCI Express card | `hmm.rst` (bandwidth, coherence, atomics), small-BAR RFC, VGA arbiter, nouveau reclocking and firmware, FreeBSD's 5.10-to-5.17 lag, Collabora's reverse engineering, LeftoverLocals / GPUHammer / Thunderclap | **Supports** the cautious wording of 3.5.2. **3.5.3**: FreeBSD's port of Linux DRM drivers is **confirmed** by its status report; the OpenBSD claim is **partly confirmed** (round 6: `drm(4)` on man.openbsd.org lists `amdgpu`, `inteldrm` and `radeondrm`, "first appeared in OpenBSD 4.3"; it does not say "ported from Linux" and lists no other vendor) |

### What round 5 changes in earlier text

* **No earlier claim was found wrong.** Two checks: (1) the 14B row on OpenBSD hybrid cores and the 14D hotplug row stand; (2) chapter 3, 3.5.3: confirmed for FreeBSD, still unconfirmed for OpenBSD.
* **The section 7 statement that DMA protection and addressing are undefined (R6)** is now backed by Thunderclap and `thunderbolt.rst` for the protection half and by `dma-isa-lpc.rst` / `mmzone.h` for the addressing half.
* **Table 7's verdict on virtual-cache aliasing (UNKNOWN)** is untouched (nothing new on it).

### Not done in round 5

* **Not reached (after the second pass, 14H):** racing the beam, HDMI audio, MST/DSC, HDCP statements, a developer statement on the PowerPC hash table, the Blit paper itself (PDF; read in round 6, 14I), the Linux 2.4 VM replacement (only a snippet), Torvalds on AVX-512 (RealWorldTech timed out), xHCI design posts (search found none), ASPM and PCIe hot-plug posts, AHCI, virtio, big.LITTLE and NUMA threads, KFD/ROCm, the OpenBSD `drm` port, simplefb/efifb sources.
* **Unverified or read in part (round 6 update in brackets):** the Ars Technica 2012 report is secondary (the video was not watched) [still so: the quotation is reported by Ars and by other press, and no transcript was found]; the GPUHammer paper only through its abstract [read in full in round 6]; the heise article only in two paragraphs [the saved text stops at an advertisement; the primary patch series was read instead]; the Thunderclap FAQ answers did not appear in the saved text [the paper was read in round 6, and the site's text was found to have been altered]; the Wayland page has no named author [still so]; the USENIX 2003 notes have no byline in the saved text [still so; the text does say that Rik van Riel gave the talk]; counts of "about 45 local files" are by hand.

## 14G. What the UNIX-HATERS Handbook complained about, and which complaints have a hardware, instruction-set or bus cause

The author asked which of the pain points in this document caused what the UNIX-HATERS Handbook (Simson Garfinkel, Daniel Weise and Steven Strassmann, eds., IDG Books / Programmers Press, 1994) complained about. **Source read:** the full-text OCR of the Internet Archive copy, `archive.org/download/TheUnixHatersHandbook/ugh_djvu.txt` (`doc0064`, 585,212 bytes, 16,746 lines; round5-index row `doc0064`). The text is OCR: some quotations carry OCR errors that I have not corrected beyond obvious line breaks; page numbers are the running page numbers visible in the text (the OCR sometimes loses them, then I give the chapter only).

**Selection.** The book was read to find the complaints that rest, wholly or partly, on hardware, instruction-set or bus design; only those are listed. Where a complaint is software design on top of a hardware cause, the row says which part is hardware. Page numbers are the running page numbers visible in the OCR (the OCR sometimes loses them; then only the chapter is given).

| Handbook complaint (chapter, page) | In the Handbook's words | Cause | Catalogued pain points that are (or are not) the cause; pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Small by rule: Unix's culture of minimalism** (ch. 1, "Unix evolution" message, quoted) | "In the early PDP-11 days, Unix programs had the following design parameters: Rule 1. It didn't have to be good, or even correct, but: Rule 2. It had to be small." and "over time, computer hardware has become progressively more powerful: processors speed up, address spaces move from 16 to 32 bits, memory gets cheaper, and so forth. So Rule 2 has been relaxed." | **Both.** The size rule has a hardware origin (the 16-bit address space of the PDP-11, as the writer says); what was done with it is design culture | Section 14 (V7 on the PDP-11 era) and the code-size principle (a). pdp64 keeps the principle but removes the limit: LP64 (39-bit virtual, 6.8.4) |
| **Terminals: the zoo of control sequences, termcap, a kernel that thinks it has a teletype** (ch. 6 "Original Sin", pp. 111-112) | "Unfortunately for us, Unix was designed in the days of teletypes."; "different manufacturers used different control sequences to accomplish similar functions"; Unix put the terminal code "into a library, but then this library, instead of being linked into the kernel where it belonged (or put in a shared library), was linked with every single application program"; "the Unix kernel still thought that it was displaying information on a conventional teletype". The chapter contrasts DEC (hard-coded VT52/VT100) and the MIT AI Lab's ITS (terminal handling in the kernel) | **Mostly software design.** The hardware fact underneath is that every terminal vendor had its own control sequences (a device-interface diversity problem). Putting that knowledge in every program was a Unix choice (the chapter says so, citing ITS as the alternative) | Not a hardware pain point of this document. Inferred link: a *serial console to an arbitrary terminal* would bring it back; pdp64's console is a polled serial device with one data register (5.5.4), and the graphics terminal is the machine's own set of memory-mapped devices (3.1), so there is no terminal-type zoo at the hardware interface. The BLT64 / framebuffer model is the answer the chapter points at: the OS owns the screen (3.1, 14F "The X server in the middle") |
| **X Windows: memory, speed, no common graphics model, NeWS lost** (ch. 7, pp. 123-142) | "How to Make a 50-MIPS Workstation Run Like a 4.77MHz IBM PC"; Open Look's clock tool "gobbles up 1.4 megabytes of real memory"; on the colour model: "The X approach to device independence is to treat everything like a Micro VAX framebuffer on acid."; on the overlay planes: using X on the graphics plane means "I cannot use the super cool 3D graphics hardware either, because in order to draw a cube, I would have to 'steal' the frame buffer from X"; for NeWS: an extensible server where "the client can download programs and data structures to draw the whole schematic" so "the user can drag an IC around smoothly, without any network traffic or context switching", which "makes it possible to run interactive clients over low-speed (that is, low-bandwidth) communication lines", and "Ultimately, NeWS was not economically or politically viable because it solved the very problems that X was designed to create." | **Mostly software and protocol, plus hardware diversity.** The hardware part is the variety of display hardware (colour maps, overlay planes, frame buffers that only one program may own); the rest is protocol and political economy, in the authors' own judgement | 14F "Old graphics pain" and "Remote display lineage": the round-trip cost of a network-transparent protocol (Sharp 2004), direct rendering, the Wayland middle-man description. A display of local devices on a bus with a descriptor ring pays no round trip. The Handbook's NeWS argument (download code to the display server so the interactive loop is local) is the same shape as the Blit lineage in chapter 3 of the manual; I did **not** find the Blit mentioned in the text I read. A NeWS author's later account (David Rosenthal, quoted by LWN, 3 Jul 2024): "I, and some of the other Sun engineers, understood that NeWS could not displace X11 as the Unix standard window system without being equally open source. But Sun's management looked at NeWS and saw superior technology, an extension of the PostScript that Adobe was selling, and couldn't bring themselves to give it away." (`doc0071`; licensing, not hardware, was the stated reason NeWS lost, which agrees with the Handbook's "economically or politically viable") |
| **Interrupted system calls: "PC loser-ing"** (Appendix C, pp. 313-314, Richard Gabriel) | "The PC [footnote 2] loser-ing problem occurs when a user program invokes a system routine to perform a lengthy operation that might have significant state ... If an interrupt occurs during the operation, the state of the user program must be saved. Because the invocation of the system routine is usually a single instruction, the PC of the user program does not adequately capture the state of the process. The system routine must either back out or press forward. The right thing is to back out and restore the user program PC to the instruction that invoked the system routine"; the Unix answer: "the solution was for the system routine to always finish, but sometimes an error code would be returned that signaled that the system routine had failed to complete its action. A correct user program, then, had to check the error code to determine whether to simply try the system routine again" | **Both.** The hardware fact: a trap into the kernel is one instruction whose saved program counter cannot describe a half-done operation, and the PDP-11 could not restart a half-executed instruction. The choice to return an error and make every program retry (EINTR) is the New Jersey choice of implementation simplicity over interface simplicity, in Gabriel's account | Catalogued causes: section 14 (V7 `trap.c` "relies on the ability of the hardware to restart a half executed instruction", `backup` routine), ch. 3. pdp64: instructions are restartable and precise, and the machine has no hidden instruction state (9.2.6, 6.8.1-6.8.2) **for faults**. Whether an *interrupted system call* restarts is a kernel decision the manual's chapter 6 leaves open (not checked beyond 6.8). So the hardware half is avoided; the software half is not decided by the hardware |
| **The file system: fast, inconsistent after a crash, `fsck`** (ch. 13 "UFS: The Root of All Evil", pp. 265-268) | "they designed it simply to run fast. As a result, the hard disk is usually in an inconsistent state."; "To ensure files are not lost or corrupted, the operations must be performed atomically and in order"; `fsck` "can take 5, 10, or 20 minutes"; "If you've been having intermittent hardware failures, SCSI termination problems, and incomplete block transfers, frequently it can't."; Andy Glew's 1990 `comp.arch` message: a DECstation 3100 "took 8:19 (eight minutes and nineteen seconds) to reboot after powercycle. That included fsck'ing the disk." | **Mostly software.** The design of UFS is blamed. The text names two hardware contributions in passing: SCSI termination (bus electrical) and incomplete block transfers. It does **not** say that disks lack atomic multi-block writes; that is my inference, marked as such | Catalogued: DMA / device-error rows (section 7; A.5 rows 161-162: posted writes, a failed device that spins drivers). pdp64: the disk interface has read, write and flush at 512-byte sectors (5.5.1); whether a multi-sector write is atomic is **not defined** (open) |
| **The swap partition** (ch. 11, p. 230) | "Early Unix didn't use the file system for swapping because the Unix file system was too slow."; the swap partition "is either too small ... or ... too large" | **Partly hardware.** The hardware part is disk (and file-system) speed in the early system, which the text gives as the reason for a fixed swap area; the fixed sizing problem is software | Catalogued: the VAX paging rows (section 14, `vmpage.c`). pdp64 has no swap design in the manual's chapters 4-6 that I checked |
| **Virtual memory behaviour** (ch. 11, p. 226-227, a Usenet message) | "Why doesn't someone tell Sun that their workstations aren't Vaxen with 2MB of RAM, it's not 1983, and there is absolutely nothing to be gained by summarily paging out stuff that you don't have to"; the cause is a kernel parameter `maxslp`, default 20 (seconds) | **Software tuning inherited from a hardware era** (VAX with 2 MB), in the writer's words | Catalogued: the VAX software accessed-bit row (section 14, `vmpage.c`). pdp64: accessed and dirty bits are software-managed too (6.8.5, errata 36), so the policy question is the kernel's |

**Reading.** Of the complaints with a hardware or instruction-set root in the text, three are plain: the **size rule** (16-bit addresses), the **interrupted system call** (a trap that cannot describe half-done work, and a CPU that could not restart half an instruction), and the **display zoo** (many framebuffer and colour models, many terminal control sequences). Three more have hardware ancestry or a hardware part (the swap partition and paging policy from small, slow machines; the file-system damage after a crash, where SCSI termination and incomplete block transfers are the hardware mentions). The most useful finding for pdp64 is Gabriel's PC loser-ing passage: it states the restartable-instruction principle (the manual's 9.2.6 and 6.8) as a design argument from 1991, and it records the Unix answer that the pdp64 hardware cannot make for the kernel.

**Counts.** One page fetched (the OCR text). Rows above: 7.

They support the other 'legacy replacement' mode where the first two comparators block interrupts from 8254 timers and from the RTC."; the comparators "share a counter, complicating resets" | `Documentation/timers/hpet.rst` | local tree | [D-local] | ch. 10: a timer whose interrupt routing is up to firmware is the 8254/RTC problem again; the pdp64 timer is in the processor's own device slot |

Found with a cable whose lanes 2 and 3 never achieve clock recovery at any rate."; "a monitor that settles on two lanes after three attempts prints nine error lines on a completely healthy attach" | Jean-Francois Bobier, `[PATCH 0/3] drm/msm/dp: fix dead link-training fallback on Type-C boards` | lore (`doc0069`) | [D] | 3.3.1, 3.3.3: link training lives in the transmitter or DisplayPort controller chip; if the chip's driver is in the kernel it is a code path that a good cable never tests |
