## 14I. Round 6: the Blit, Research Unix and Plan 9: primary sources

Round 5 had only the abstract of the Blit paper and said so (14F, "Remote display lineage"). Round 6 (23 Oct 2026) read the papers themselves. **Method that worked** (labelled **[P]** paper by the designers, **[M]** manual page, **[D]** developer message, **[S]** secondary): the browser's `#fetch` of a PDF yields no text (the built-in viewer has no DOM text), and the earlier attempt through docslib returned 137 bytes. What worked: fetch a normal page on the *same host* (`doc.cat-v.org`, `www.cs.tufts.edu`, `9p.io`), then run a `#js` directive that downloads the file with a synchronous XMLHttpRequest from inside that page (same origin, so no cross-site block), turns the bytes into base64, writes the base64 into the page body, and `#save` stores the page text; the base64 is decoded on disk and `pdftotext -layout` (PDFs) or `gunzip` and `ps2ascii` (PostScript) extracts the text. One request per file, 20 s apart. The archive.org route did not work: its metadata search found one video item ("Blit Terminal (1982)") and the journal scan `sim_software-practice-experience_1985-02_15_2` answered `401 Authorization Required`. Round 5 failed because it treated "PDF" as "not readable" instead of finding a way to get the bytes onto disk; the material was on cat-v.org, on a Tufts course archive and on 9p.io all along. Every page and file is listed in [`round6-index.tsv`](codegen-data/kernelpain/round6-index.tsv).

**Papers read in full or in the parts named** (page numbers are those of the saved text):

| Short name | Paper | Read |
|---|---|---|
| Blit 1983 | Rob Pike, "The Blit: A Multiplexed Graphics Terminal", AT&T Bell Laboratories Technical Journal 63(8), Oct 1984; the preprint is dated "Sometime in 1983" | all 15 pages |
| Tradeoffs 1985 | Rob Pike, Bart Locanthi, John Reiser, "Hardware/Software Tradeoffs for Bitmap Graphics on the Blit", Software: Practice and Experience 15(2), Feb 1985 (the 9p.io copy is dated 30 Mar 2000) | all but the middle of the graphics-input section, which I skimmed |
| Layers 1983 | Rob Pike, "Graphics in Overlapping Bitmap Layers", Computing Science Technical Report 999, 1 Apr 1983 (ACM TOG 2(2), 1983) | introduction and comparison with previous work; the algorithm sections skimmed |
| Transparent 1988 | Rob Pike, "Window Systems Should Be Transparent", Computing Systems 1(3), 1988 | all |
| Concurrent 1989 | Rob Pike, "A Concurrent Window System", Computing Systems 2(2), 1989 | introduction, comparison and basics; the rest skimmed |
| 8½ 1991 | Rob Pike, "8½, the Plan 9 Window System", USENIX Summer 1991 (the cat-v text is the version with the 1992 status note) | all |
| Plan 9 | Pike, Presotto, Thompson, Trickey, Winterbottom, "Plan 9 from Bell Labs" (cat-v 4th edition text) | the sections on terminals, the window system and 9P, by keyword; not read end to end |
| Manuals | rio(1), draw(3) of Plan 9; mux(9.1) and 32ld(9.1) of the Tenth Edition Research Unix manual | all four pages |
| Mailing list | TUHS, thread "Blit source", 15-19 Dec 2019: 9 messages (Pike 3, Ruizendaal 2, Papenhoff 1, Haertel 1, Cross 1, Robbins 1 quoting Pike) | those messages; the other 17 of the 26 in the thread not read |

**Not read:** "The Use of Name Spaces in Plan 9" and the rio paper itself (cat-v has only slides, `rio_slides.pdf`, not fetched); Locanthi's or Pike's own writing on the Jerq before the Blit; the 5620 schematics on bitsavers (cited by Ruizendaal, not opened); the 1982 demonstration video; Pike's own pages (herpolhode.com) and Hacker News were not tried (the sources above answered the questions). Wiley's abstract of the Tradeoffs paper was seen only as a search snippet.

### I.1 The hardware

| Fact | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CPU, memory, display | "256K bytes of memory dual-ported between an 800×1024×1 bit display and a Motorola MC68000 microprocessor, with 24K of ROM, an RS-232 interface, a mouse and a keyboard. Unlike many graphics terminals, it has no special-purpose graphics hardware; instead, the microprocessor executes all graphical operations in software." Clock: "a Motorola MC68000 running at 8 MHz" | Pike, 1983 (Blit); Pike, Locanthi, Reiser, 1985 (Tradeoffs) | Blit 1983 p. 1; Tradeoffs, "Overview" | [P] | the Blit had **no blitter**: 3.1.1's "bit-block-transfer engine beside it" is not the Blit's (see I.7) |
| No graphics engine at all | "The Blit has no special graphics memory, no display list processor, no hardware for moving the mouse cursor, and no pixel-moving hardware." | Tradeoffs, "Overview" | Tradeoffs | [P] | same |
| One address space, framebuffer in it | "The remaining 156 Kbytes are used for program and data, but the division of the address space is imposed entirely by software: there is only one address space, shared by the processor and the display. The CPU executes instructions from the display memory, because that is the only RAM in the terminal." About 100 Kbytes of the 256 Kbytes are scanned to the display at 30 Hz (60 Hz interlaced) | Tradeoffs, "Overview" | Tradeoffs | [P] | supports 3.1.2's framebuffer in the address space; the pdp64 framebuffer is uncached, the Blit's was plain RAM with no cache at all |
| Two display registers | "There are two registers in the display hardware. One sets the base address of the display, so the program can display any part of memory, and the other determines how bits in the memory are displayed." | Tradeoffs, "Overview" | Tradeoffs | [P] | a base-address register is what 14F proposed (a vblank-latched base register); here is the Blit's own precedent (the Blit's register was not said to be latched at vertical blanking) |
| Cost of dual-ported video | "Video refresh takes approximately a third of the RAMs' bandwidth."; "The fraction of memory cycles lost to the video depends greatly on the application", from about 10% for CPU-intensive tasks to perhaps 50% for memory-intensive tasks, because the 16-bit data bus needs two fetches for a 32-bit quantity The arbiter: "An asynchronous arbiter controls access to the memory: if the memory is busy, the requesting entity is suspended until it is freed". When the processor waits it waits "about 500ns" | Tradeoffs, "Hardware I: Memory" | Tradeoffs | [P] | the scan-out master takes bus bandwidth from the processor: 14F's fixed bus priority for the video master and the line buffer are the pdp64 answers; the numbers belong to a 1983 bus |
| Memory width | 32-bit-wide RAM from thirty-two 64K by 1-bit chips; the 68000 bus is 16 bits wide | Tradeoffs, "Hardware I: Memory" | Tradeoffs | [P] | context |
| ROM | 24 K of ROM holds "the graphics primitives and interrupt-level I/O drivers"; they "execute out of ROM, they are not down-loaded" | Blit 1983 p. 4 | Blit 1983 | [P] | boot (chapter 4): the Blit's boot is "a small multi-process operating system is loaded into the terminal" from the host over the serial line |
| Serial link and speed | one RS-232 line; "which takes a few seconds at 19200 baud and about two minutes at 1200 baud" to load the terminal operating system; office terminals ran at 19200 baud, home dial-up at 1200; the 1988 paper says "A single serial line, running typically at 9600bps" | Pike 1983; Pike 1988 | Blit 1983 pp. 3-4; Transparent 1988, "The Model" | [P] | the pdp64 terminal has no serial link to its host; the descriptor ring (chapter 5) carries what the wire carried |
| The 68000 and the mouse | the mouse position is two registers: "The mouse coordinates are therefore asynchronously updated variables in the program's address space. The high bit of each register indicates whether current data is valid"; position is read at the 60 Hz vertical-retrace interrupt; the buttons "generate an interrupt when their state changes" | Tradeoffs, graphics-input section | Tradeoffs | [P] | the vblank interrupt as the cursor clock (14F, vblank rows) |
| Protection | "Read or write references to the first 8 bytes of the processor's address space generate an interrupt" that the kernel catches; and "the hardware provides no memory management" | Blit 1983 p. 11 | Blit 1983 | [P] | a null-pointer trap without an MMU; chapter 6 has an MMU, so this is background only |
| UARTs, from the schematics | "a M68K cpu, ram, rom, two 6850 UARTS and the circuit to drive the display"; the later 5620 "appears to be replaced by a single 2681 programmable DUART" and has "an I/O expansion port and a bit of non-volatile memory" | Paul Ruizendaal (inference from the 5620 schematics and the papers), 18 and 19 Dec 2019 | TUHS 019645, 019652 | [D], inferred by the poster | context; he suggests that an FPGA Blit with 720p HDMI output "would not be impossible to do" (a poster's idea, not a result) |
| Z-80 | **not found in any text read**. The Blit paper says Motorola MC68000; a Blit predecessor "built by Dave Ditzel" is described only as a terminal programmed with escape sequences (CPU not named) | | Blit 1983 p. 4 | | the "Z-80" in the round-6 instruction is unconfirmed; the author did not put it in chapter 3 |

### I.2 The software model: host, terminal, protocol, what travels

| Fact | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| The terminal is a multi-process machine, not a display list | "a small multi-process operating system is loaded into the terminal, and the processes under that operating system are then loaded. The operating system is structured around asynchronous overlapping windows, called layers." On the host the Unix program `mpx` and in the terminal `mpxterm`: "A user-level Unix program, mpx, communicates with a small real-time multi-process operating system, mpxterm, running in the terminal" | Blit 1983 pp. 1, 9 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the Blit's terminal was a computer running its own small operating system; pdp64's terminal is devices on the bus, not a second computer |
| What is downloaded | "The microprocessor may be loaded from the host with custom applications software, but the terminal is rarely used this way. Instead, a small multi-process operating system is loaded". Programs: "Programs are loaded into the Blit from the host computer's disc by a user program on Unix that communicates with a special program load process in the terminal." The program is relocated on the host: "down-loaded programs are relocated in the host to an address returned by alloc in the Blit" | Blit 1983 pp. 1, 11-13 | Blit 1983 | [P] | **code travels, confirmed** |
| The load protocol and its speed | "After each 64 bytes of data sent, an acknowledgement packet from the terminal arrives"; at 19200 baud "the RS-232 line is almost saturated, delivering over 16000 user bits per second into the terminal and consuming 70% of a VAX-11/750"; "the text editor, which is about 10K bytes long, is relocated in 3 seconds and downloads in about 6 seconds at 19200 baud"; "no other version of the system on the same hardware can deliver down-loaded programs faster than about 6000 baud" | Blit 1983 pp. 10-11 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the cost of moving code over a slow link, in numbers |
| The protocol on the wire | "The protocol multiplexes I/O on the single RS-232 cable from the terminal to the host." Each message is "prefixed by a header"; `mpx` adds "a tag specifying which layer will receive the data and a cyclic redundancy check for error detection and recovery"; "about half of [1600 lines] implement the error-correcting protocol"; the terminal needs "no flow control" because "An error-corrected protocol with flow control is required." | Blit 1983 pp. 4, 8-10 | Blit 1983 | [P] | an error-corrected, flow-controlled, multiplexed packet stream: the wire needed what the bus gets from handshakes; the 5.x ring needs none of this |
| Pixels were **not** sent | "Programs in the terminal have access to an extensive bitmap graphics library"; all drawing is in the terminal: "the microprocessor executes all graphical operations in software". What crosses the wire is characters and application messages: the editor `jim` "The two programs communicate by a protocol consisting essentially of 'insert string' and 'delete string' message packets and requests for data"; the debugger `joff` uses "remote procedure calls"; "the Unix program knows nothing about the appearance of the display." Where the paper discusses pixel traffic it is to avoid it: "RS-232 is not fast enough for file I/O. The text editor must be written in two parts, using the terminal much like a cache." | Blit 1983 pp. 6, 14 | Blit 1983 | [P] | **the serial-link statement is half right: code yes, pixels no** (see I.7) |
| Why two halves | "Unfortunately, writing one program in two pieces is much harder than writing two programs. Still, we don't want local disc." | Blit 1983 p. 14 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the cost of a terminal/host split, as the author of the Blit saw it |
| No window manager | "There is no 'window manager' in the conventional sense bitblt is the window interface." A process's layer is an "ordinary bitmap, obscured or not" | Blit 1983 p. 12 | Blit 1983 | [P] | graphics as one primitive on bitmaps; layers (windows that overlap) are handled below the application |
| System calls | "system calls are indirect subroutine calls through a vector at a known location. The execution penalty is only one extra instruction for a system call compared to an ordinary procedure call." Processes are scheduled round-robin and switch only on request: "there is no preemptive scheduling." Size: `mpxterm` "is 1627 lines of C" plus "204 lines of assembler" | Blit 1983 pp. 10-13 | Blit 1983 | [P] | cooperative, no MMU: the opposite of chapter 6; context only |
| Host side on Research Unix | "The original mpx was written using Greg Chesson's file multiplexing facilities in 7th Edition Unix. On Unix System V, the IPC for mpx is provided by a kernel driver written by Piers Dick-Lauder. The mpx running on the author's machine exploits the user-level IPC in the character I/O system of 8th Edition Unix." The 8th Edition uses streams ("a Stream Input-Output System", Ritchie, same issue) and pseudo-terminal pairs `/dev/pt` | Blit 1983 p. 9 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the host-side half is a use of Research Unix streams, not a new device |

### I.3 The trade-offs the authors state

| Trade-off | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Why a terminal at all | the idea was "to provide a graphics machine with about the power of the Xerox Alto" using 1981 technology (large address space microprocessors, 64K RAMs and programmed array logic) "to keep size, complexity and particularly cost much lower" | Blit 1983 p. 2 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the purpose was an affordable Alto, not a protocol |
| Why RS-232, not a network | "Because we refuse to have rotating machinery in our offices, we wanted to build the Blit around a network interface rather than a disc. But after several lengthy discussions we decided that network hardware and software were not yet inexpensive, available or reliable enough to be the center of a workstation (this was early in 1981, and the situation now (mid 1983) is hardly better)."; "much of the Blit's success can be attributed to the use of RS-232" | Blit 1983 pp. 2-3 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the serial line was a cost and availability decision, not a design ideal |
| Why smart | "To draw graphics in the windows, sending escape sequences is traditional but makes poor use of the processing power of the terminal, and requires the terminal to be pre-programmed with all desired capabilities."; "Contrary to popular usage, an intelligent terminal is not an idiot savant, it is one that can be educated." | Blit 1983 p. 4 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the answer to "why did code travel": the terminal could be taught |
| The failed first attempt | the Ditzel predecessor interpreted escape sequences: "the software to generate the incremental control information ... was messy too much state information was maintained by the terminal and guessed at by the Unix program"; "Putting all data into labeled packets eliminates this confusion and obviates optimization." | Blit 1983 p. 4 | Blit 1983 | [P] | a protocol with hidden state was the first pain; packets with a type and a channel fixed it |
| Low bandwidth is the worst problem | "Although the graphics is fast enough, the hardware isn't big enough: memory is tight when working on big programs, and there isn't enough offscreen bitmap storage. The greatest problem, though, is certainly the low bandwidth." And the choice they would keep: "we prefer increasing the memory and bandwidth, leaving the basic structure the same, to adding disc and therefore expense, noise, and the proliferation of local copies of software." | Blit 1983 p. 14 | Blit 1983 | [P] | the pdp64 terminal removes the wire; the memory pain (off-screen storage) is the block-RAM budget of 3.4 |
| Hardware and software designed together | "Perhaps the most important lesson from the Blit is that hardware and software should be designed together, by people that are going to use it."; "Carefully designed software can outperform infelicitous hardware." | Tradeoffs, "Conclusions" | Tradeoffs | [P] | the pdp64 design principle in the project's own terms |
| Hardware assist, advice | "Sometimes, of course, a designer is faced with demands for utmost speed for a particular application. This may require direct hardware support, but such support should be provided in a way that enables efficient, general software to be written for the device. One way to do this is to make the hardware transparent, so it can be bypassed when the software wants direct access to the display." | Tradeoffs, "Conclusions" | Tradeoffs | [P] | **directly on BLT64**: a blitter beside a plain framebuffer in the address space is exactly this; a blitter that the software cannot bypass would be the case the authors warn against |
| Why no barrel shifter | an early Blit design had a hardware barrel shifter: "We estimated the barrel shifter would speed up character drawing at most 30%, which was too small an effect to warrant the extra hardware complication and expense. Our feeling was that hardware support should assist all bitblt calls uniformly." | Tradeoffs, "Hardware II: Other systems" | Tradeoffs | [P] | the test BLT64 should pass: assist every case or the common ones, and say which |
| Where hardware could help most | "For large areas, bitblt on the Blit is within a factor of two of what the memory can support, the missing cycles being consumed by instruction fetches. Therefore, the forms of hardware assistance that can help the most involve raw speed: speeding up the memory or (to a lesser extent) the processor." | Tradeoffs, "Hardware II: Other systems" | Tradeoffs | [P] | for big rectangles the memory is the limit; a DMA-style blitter helps by freeing the processor, not by beating the memory |
| Cost of the software bitblt | "The complete bitblt subroutine is 1524 bytes of code, and can scroll the screen one pixel vertically in 0.13 seconds, and horizontally in 0.38 seconds."; characters: "the Blit draws about 2500 characters per second in the standard font, which is 9 pixels (not 8) by 14"; "over half the total time to draw a character on the Blit goes into overhead" | Tradeoffs, "Integration" and "Software III" | Tradeoffs | [P] | the numbers a 1983 processor needed; BLT64's job is to make these free of processor time |
| How `bitblt` was written | five versions; version 5 "compiles optimal code on-the-fly for each invocation, then jumps to the generated code"; "In the worst case, bitblt executes 400 instructions to generate the optimal sequence"; "The size of the generated code ranges from 16 to 72 bytes. If all the cases were written out (including all the shifts and Codes) then the total size would approach one megabyte"; the table counts "1944 lower bound on cases" | Tradeoffs, "Software III: Blit bitblt" | Tradeoffs | [P] | the case explosion BLT64's registers must hide; a hardware blitter with strides, shifts and a raster-operation field collapses the 1944 cases into register fields |
| Raster operations needed | "Of the 16 dyadic boolean functions, these four have proven sufficient for most applications." The four are STORE, OR, CLR, XOR; "most characters on the Blit are drawn in XOR mode, because it is about 10% faster" and "avoids interaction with the mouse cursor" | Tradeoffs, "Software II: Primitives" | Tradeoffs | [P] | 3.1.4 lists sixteen binary and common ternary operations; the Blit shows four are enough and XOR is the one that matters |
| One-dimensional memory | "memory is one dimensional, and any two-dimensionality of interpretation is enforced by software, not hardware"; "If memory were two-dimensional, balloc would require a 2-D bin packing algorithm, and bitmaps wider than the display could not be created." | Tradeoffs, "Hardware I: Memory" | Tradeoffs | [P] | a stride register in BLT64 (3.1.4) is the same idea in hardware; the Blit's `width` field made a layer share the screen's width: "this trick of sharing the width creates a software version of two-dimensional memory" |
| Other hardware compared | Sun 1: separate 1024×1024 display memory "reached through the 16-bit interface on the Multibus": "bitblt must execute 4 different inner loops for the various combinations of source and destination in main memory or the display"; "we know of no complete bitblt implementation for the SUN 1"; Apollo DN400 "bit mover" is "STORE mode only, and only works on the display memory" with "roughly a factor of ten performance difference between otherwise identical STORE and XOR operations"; Sun 2: "The SUN 2 hardware is, however, considerably more complex and expensive than either the SUN 1 or the Blit." | Tradeoffs, "Hardware II: Other systems" | Tradeoffs | [P] | a blitter that works only on display memory, or only for STORE, is the trap BLT64 avoids if its source and destination may be anywhere in the address space (3.1.4 says base addresses, so yes) |
| Timing table | scroll vertically: Sun 1 187 ms, Sun 2 109 ms (display) and 82.2 ms (memory), Blit 129 ms; horizontally 194, 110, 311, 376; letter 'a' at random positions 1.1, 0.34, 0.74, 0.42; texturing a random 40×40 square 1.89, 0.82, 1.78, 1.60 | Tradeoffs Table 2 | Tradeoffs | [P] | one table of 1983 numbers; the column order is as printed (Sun 1, Sun 2 display, Sun 2 memory, Blit) |
| Cursor in software | "Some graphics systems provide circuitry to place the mouse cursor in the video signal instead of the memory, to avoid interference with graphics operations, while others provide no hardware at all. The Blit lies in the middle"; the cursor is removed on non-XOR drawing and redrawn at the next clock interrupt, "Although this makes the cursor flicker during heavy non-XOR activity" | Tradeoffs, graphics input | Tradeoffs | [P] | 3.1 has no cursor plane; a sprite (the C64 had them) is the hardware answer the Blit did without |
| Memory-latency trick on another machine | the Ridge computer used the "page modified bit" so that a shadow memory is copied to the display only when written; the Sun 2 refreshes the tube from a shadow memory that watches the CPU's writes | Tradeoffs, "Hardware II: Other systems" | Tradeoffs | [P] | the dual-port cost (a third of the RAM bandwidth) had two known hardware answers in 1983; the pdp64 line buffer is a third |

### I.4 Research Unix: the Blit in the Computing Science Research Center

| Fact | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Who | "Most important among them is Bart Locanthi, who designed and built the terminal and much of the underlying graphics software."; "wrote the error-correcting protocol in mpx" (Piers Dick-Lauder); "mpx and mpxterm were written by one (Pike)." Others thanked: Sally Browning, Tom Cargill, Greg Chesson, Joe Condon, Dave Ditzel, Steve Johnson, Andrew Hume, John Reiser and Dennis Ritchie | Blit 1983 pp. 14-15 | Blit 1983 | [P] | attribution for chapter 3 |
| The Blit sprang from a Unix goal | "We therefore began thinking about using the Blit to improve the Unix environment, rather than replace or even merely add to it."; "The Blit has taught us that multiprogramming has been underused."; "The Unix system really knows nothing about the multiplexing going on; the user is just running more processes than usual." | Blit 1983 pp. 3, 14 | Blit 1983 | [P] | context: the window system is a user program, not a kernel feature |
| Names and sequence | "The Jerq was the original name for the 68K machines hand-made by Bart. The name, originally coined for a fun demo of the Three Rivers Perq by folks at Lucasfilm, was borrowed with permission by us but was considered unsuitable by Sam Morgan as we reached out to make some industrially, by a company (something Atlantic) on Long Island. So 'Blit' was coined."; "The sequence is thus Jerq, Blit, DMD-5620."; the 5620 "had a Western Electric BellMac 32000 CPU" | Rob Pike, TUHS, 19 Dec 2019 | TUHS 019653 | [D] (Pike, from memory, 36 years later) | the hand-made 68000 machine was the Jerq, the Blit was the industrial version, the DMD 5620 (Teletype) the product; Wikipedia's account is looser (I.6) |
| Which tape holds what | "The V8 tape has the Blit stuff in /usr/blit, and the 5620 stuff in /usr/jerq."; Papenhoff: "both the 68k and 32000 blit stuff is on the v8 tape (at least binaries) and both work. 68k is running fine under emulation"; "the up-to-date version of the code was kept in /usr/jerq" is his theory; V10 has a 630 directory | Mike Haertel, 19 Dec 2019; Angelo Papenhoff, 19 Dec 2019 | TUHS 019654, 019657 | [D], partly a guess by the poster | the 8th Edition manual set is the nearest primary text for mux |
| C compiler | "The 68000 compiler was pcc and was under fairly continuous development during this project." The tape's README: "the source for /usr/blit/lib/ccom has been lost." | Rob Pike, TUHS, 18 Dec 2019 (the README quoted by Ruizendaal) | TUHS 019645 | [D] | the Blit's compiler was the portable C compiler, as the pdp64's is |
| `mux` in the Tenth Edition | "Mux manages asynchronous windows, or layers."; "Each layer is essentially a separate terminal. Characters typed into the layer are sent to the standard input of a Unix process bound to the layer"; it is described as "layer multiplexer for 5620" and `32ld` as "bootstrap loader for 5620": "32ld loads the MAC-32 object file for execution in a 5620 connected to the standard output." The man page cites "R. Pike, `Blit Download Protocols', this manual, Vol. 2" (not read) | Tenth Edition manual, mux(9.1), 32ld(9.1) | `man.cat-v.org/unix_10th/9/mux` | [M] | the 10th Edition tools are for the 5620 (Bellmac 32), not the 68000 Blit |
| The window system as a Unix program | "Mux is a window system with no icons, no help facility, no customizability, no noise, and only two menus (one with five entries, one with seven)."; "terminal for programmers, such as myself"; the author uses the UNIX system himself; "the idea of a programmable terminal is more a byproduct of the way mux developed than a conscious design decision." | Pike, 1988 | Transparent 1988, "Introduction", "The Model" | [P] | the programmable terminal was a by-product, said its author |
| Windows without cursor addressing | "Mux windows do not support cursor addressing. This was a deliberate omission; I wanted to break cleanly away from 'visual' software to tools that use the mouse." "when it was made in 1981 it was extremely contentious." | Pike, 1988 | Transparent 1988, "System issues" | [P] | no VT-style control sequences in the graphics path; the terminal's text window is a text stream |
| `mux` ran on obsolete hardware, X won | "Mux runs only on slow, obsolete hardware that is no longer manufactured, while X runs on almost every modern display. It seems likely I'll soon be asked to turn off my mux machine and start using X."; "X needs a much smoother window manager." | Pike, 1988 | Transparent 1988, "Introduction" | [P] | Pike's own view in 1988 of the relation to X: a competitor, not a successor |

### I.5 Plan 9: from the Blit to 8½ to rio

| Fact | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8½ as the Blit's descendant | "Primarily for reasons of history and familiarity, the general model and appearance of 8½ are similar to those of mux." "The default program that runs in a window is familiar to users of Blit terminals" | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991, "User's Model" | [P] | the lineage Blit, mux, 8½ is stated by the author of all three |
| rio as 8½'s descendant | "Descendant of 8½"; "8½ originally written in C, using processes and hand-built coroutines based on setjmp and longjmp"; "For Brazil, rewritten from scratch in Alef in 1995, and converted to true threads and procs. Converted (in an afternoon) in 1999 to use the thread library."; the 8½ paper's note: "Note that 8½ has been replaced by rio (see rio(1))." rio(1): "Rio manages asynchronous layers of text, or windows, on a raster display." | Pike (rio notes); Plan 9 manual | `doc.cat-v.org/plan_9/3rd_edition/rio/`; rio(1) | [P], [M] | the word "layers" survived from the Blit to rio's manual page |
| What Plan 9 terminals are | "The terminals are comparable to modest workstations ... Unlike workstations, however, their role is just to provide an affordable multiplexed user interface to the rest of the system: they run the window system and support simple interactive tasks such as text editing. Thus they lie somewhere between workstations and X terminals in design, cost, performance, and function." The Gnot "a 68020-based machine"; Pike in 2019 said "The Gnot had a 68040 ... Two bits per pixel" | Pike, 8½ paper, 1991/92; Pike, TUHS, 19 Dec 2019 | 8½ 1991; TUHS 019656 | [P], [D] | **the two accounts differ on the Gnot's CPU (68020 in 1992, 68040 in 2019); I report both**; Pike qualifies the 2019 recollection himself ("if I remember right, but I probably don't" is about the network speed) |
| The design move: the window system is a file server | "It provides textual I/O and bitmap graphic services to both local and remote client programs by offering a multiplexed file service to those clients."; "The most important contributor to its small size, though, is its overall design as a file server." The entire interface "is provided through reading and writing files in /dev": `/dev/cons`, `/dev/mouse`, `/dev/screen`, `/dev/window`, `/dev/bitblt` | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991 | [P] | **why Plan 9 moved the window system into the file system: size and remote use, in the author's words** |
| What the files do | `/dev/mouse`: "a ten-byte message encoding the state of the buttons and the position of the cursor" (`'m'`, a byte of buttons, 4 bytes x, 4 bytes y, low byte first); `/dev/bitblt`: "It implements a protocol that encodes bitmap graphics operations. Most of the messages in the protocol (there are 23 messages in all, about half to manage the multi-level fonts necessary for efficient handling of Unicode characters) are transmissions (via a write) from the client to the window system"; the bitblt message is `'b'`, 2 bytes destination id, 2×4 bytes destination point, 2 bytes source id, 4×4 bytes source rectangle, 2 bytes function code | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991, "Graphical input", "Graphical output" | [P] | a command stream written to a device: the shape of a descriptor ring entry |
| Bitmaps live in the server | "the clients do not store the actual data for any of their bitmaps locally. Instead, the protocol provides a message to allocate a bitmap, to be stored in the server, and returns to the client an integer identifier, much like a UNIX file descriptor"; "no bitmap graphics operations are executed in the client at all; they are all performed on its behalf by the server"; this "permits remote machines having no graphics capability, such as the CPU server, to run graphics applications"; the "Analogous features of the original Andrew window system" and of X "require more complex mechanisms" | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991 | [P] | in the Blit the host sent no pixels; in 8½ the host sends drawing commands that name server-side bitmaps: the same economy by another route |
| Multiplexing by simulation | "Thus the environment in which 8½ runs has exactly the structure it provides for its clients; 8½ reproduces the environment for its clients, multiplexing the interface to keep the clients separate."; the window system "may run in one of its own windows" | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991 | [P] | the same device interface at every level; chapter 5's uniform eight-register face is a hardware version of this |
| Per-window private files | "Each window also has a private bitmap and multiplexed access to the keyboard, mouse, and other graphical resources through files like /dev/mouse, /dev/bitblt, and /dev/cons (analogous to UNIX's /dev/tty)." "Unlike X windows, where a new application typically creates a new window to run in, an 8½ graphics application usually runs in the window where it starts." and "a remote 8½ application sees the mouse, bitblt, and cons files for the window as usual in /dev; it does not know whether the files are local." | Pike et al., "Plan 9 from Bell Labs" | Plan 9 paper, "The Command-level View" and "The File Server" | [P] | the per-process name space is why there is no tty driver and no separate protocol; pdp64 has no name space in the manual yet (see chapter 6) |
| Size and speed | the whole system, with its terminal program, is "well under 90 kilobytes of text on a Motorola 68020 processor", "about half the size of the operating system kernel that supports it" and "a tenth the size of the X server"; benchmarks against X11 release 4: "circles and arcs are drawn about half as fast in 8½ as in X11 release 4", "Line drawing speed is about equal", "the bitblt test is runs four times faster for 8½"; source "totaling 5100 lines", all of 8½ "from the lowest levels to the highest" 14651 lines | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991, "Implementation" | [P] | benchmark of 1991 on a 68020; the author's own, not independent |
| `/dev/draw` in rio-era Plan 9 | "The draw device serves a three-level file system providing an interface to the graphics facilities of the system. Each client of the device connects by opening /dev/draw/new"; files `ctl`, `data`, `colormap`, `refresh`; "The format of messages written to data is a single letter followed by binary parameters; multibyte integers are transmitted with the low order byte first." "All graphics requests are clipped to their images." | draw(3), Plan 9 manual | `man.cat-v.org/plan_9/3/draw` | [M] | the successor of `/dev/bitblt`: still a message stream on a device file |
| Terminal and CPU server split | "Heavy computations such as compilation, text processing, or scientific calculation are done on the CPU servers ... For interactive work, these computations can access the terminal that instantiated them."; "Plan 9 users do their computing on the CPU servers." | Pike, 8½ paper | 8½ 1991 | [P] | the Blit's split (terminal for interaction, host for computing) survived in Plan 9 as terminal and CPU server |

### I.6 Lineage claims: who says what

| Claim | Who says it | Source | Label | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| The Blit (mpx, mux) influenced 8½ and rio | Pike (8½ paper: "similar to those of mux"); Wikipedia: "The design of these influenced the later Plan 9 window systems 8½ and rio." | 8½ 1991; `en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blit_(computer_terminal)` | [P], [S] | **confirmed** by the designer |
| The Blit was a precursor of X11 | **No source read says it.** Pike in 1988 sets mux against X ("X needs a much smoother window manager"); Wikipedia's Blit article does not mention X11. One real connection, stated by Pike in 2019 and quoted by Robbins: "The 'render extension' in X Windows originated there [the Gnot, Plan 9], after an epiphany I had while watching Hoop Dreams. True story." (a later Plan 9 terminal and a later X extension, not the Blit and the X11 protocol) | Transparent 1988; TUHS 019656, 019658 | [P], [D], [S] | **unconfirmed** for the Blit; the Gnot-to-Render link is Pike's own account |
| The Blit was a precursor of NeWS | **No source read says it.** Pike, 1989, on his own Newsqueak window system: "The external structure of the system is akin to that of NeWS. Remotely executing applications implement graphical user interfaces by connecting to the window system over the network and calling upon the Newsqueak interpreter to execute code on their behalf." The Wikipedia article NeWS (read) does not mention the Blit. The shape (download code to the display so the interaction loop is local) is common to the Blit and NeWS; whether one led to the other is not stated | Concurrent 1989; Wikipedia NeWS (no match) | [P], [S] | **unconfirmed as lineage; confirmed as similar shape** (the same shape as the Handbook's NeWS argument in 14G) |
| The Blit received code and pixels over a serial line | see I.2: code yes (RS-232, 19200 or 1200 baud); pixels no | Blit 1983 | [P] | **half confirmed**: see I.7 |
| The Blit was a Motorola 68000 machine | Pike, Locanthi, Reiser: "a Motorola MC68000 running at 8 MHz"; Pike 2019: the Jerq was "the original, Locanthi-built and -designed 68K machines" | Tradeoffs; TUHS 019653 | [P], [D] | **confirmed** for the Jerq and Blit; the DMD 5620 product used a Bellmac 32 (WE32100) |
| Wikipedia's CPU statement | "The 5620 used a Western Electric 32100 processor ... The 630 and 730 had Motorola 68000 processors" | Wikipedia Blit article | [S] | **not checked against a primary source for the 630 and 730**; Pike says the 5620 had a Bellmac 32000 CPU, which agrees; the 68000 was the original Jerq and Blit, which Wikipedia does not say |
| mux is the successor of mpx | Wikipedia: "Pike wrote two window systems for the Blit, mpx for 8th Edition Unix and mux for 9th Edition". The Blit paper says `mpx` was written for several Unix hosts, including the 8th Edition version by Dick-Lauder ("the 8th Edition version of mpx") | Wikipedia; Blit 1983 | [S], [P] | **partly contradicted**: the 8th Edition `mpx` was by Dick-Lauder with Pike's design, according to the paper; the paper does not mention `mux` at all |
| "layers" as a product | Wikipedia: "When the Blit was commercialized as the DMD 5620, a variant of mpx called 'layers' was added to SVR3." | Wikipedia | [S], not checked | unconfirmed (the paper uses "layers" for the windows, and a 1983 report with that title exists: Layers 1983) |

### I.7 Verdict on the author's account (chapter 3, 3.1.1 and its commentary)

The author's account, as recorded in `03-graphics.md`: the Blit "was a Motorola 68000 machine that received code and pixels from its host over a serial link, and a precursor of the X11 protocol and of the NeWS window system", with "a framebuffer in the address space plus a bit-block-transfer engine".

| Part of the account | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Motorola 68000 | **Confirmed** | Tradeoffs, "Overview"; TUHS 019653 |
| terminal that receives from its host over a serial link | **Confirmed** (RS-232, 1200 to 19200 baud; "a regular terminal with an RS-232 EIA port to a time-shared host") | Blit 1983 p. 3 |
| code | **Confirmed**: the terminal operating system and each application are downloaded, relocated on the host | Blit 1983 pp. 10-14 |
| pixels | **Contradicted in this form**: the terminal drew all pixels itself; the wire carried characters, editing messages and remote procedure calls, because "RS-232 is not fast enough" for bulk data | Blit 1983 pp. 6, 14 |
| framebuffer in the address space | **Confirmed**: "there is only one address space, shared by the processor and the display" | Tradeoffs, "Overview" |
| bit-block-transfer **engine** | **Contradicted**: the Blit had "no pixel-moving hardware"; `bitblt` was 1524 bytes of 68000 code; a barrel shifter was designed and dropped. The operation is the Blit's; the engine is the pdp64's addition | Tradeoffs, "Overview", "Software III", "Hardware II" |
| precursor of X11 | **Unconfirmed** (no source read says so; Pike treats X as a competitor in 1988) | I.6 |
| precursor of NeWS | **Unconfirmed as lineage**; the shape is the same (downloaded code, local interaction loop) | I.6 |
| the Blit and 8½ and rio are one line | **Confirmed** (Pike, 8½ paper; rio notes; Wikipedia) | I.5 |

### I.8 What this means for the pdp64 graphics chapter

| pdp64 item | Finding | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| The C64-plus-Blit terminal model (3.1.1) | The Blit and the pdp64 terminal agree on the memory model (one address space, framebuffer in it, uniform bitmaps, no special graphics memory) and on hardware and software designed together. They differ in the machine: the Blit was a second computer driven over a wire; the pdp64 terminal is devices on the bus. That difference removes the Blit's two worst costs (low bandwidth, writing programs in two halves) and its whole protocol layer | **Supports the memory model; the "precursor of X11 and NeWS" statement is the author's, not found in the sources** |
| BLT64 (3.1.4) | The Blit shows the operation is the right primitive (a handful of calls cover characters, scrolling, windows), that four raster operations suffice and XOR dominates, that a stride or width field replaces a two-dimensional memory, and that the case count of an unaided software implementation is large (1944 cases, 400 instructions to generate code). It also shows the authors **declined** hardware assist for cost and for uniformity ("assist all bitblt calls uniformly"), and warns that hardware assist should be "transparent, so it can be bypassed". BLT64 is therefore a pdp64 decision that goes beyond the Blit, consistent with the Blit authors' conditions, not a copy of a Blit feature | **Supports with a correction of the lineage wording**; add a software-fallback rule (the framebuffer remains plain memory the processor can draw into) |
| Video device (3.1.2) | Framebuffer as ordinary memory, scanned out by a second master that costs a third of the bandwidth (Blit); one base-address register; absolute mouse registers read at the vblank interrupt; cursor drawn by software in XOR | **Supports**; adds the Blit's measured cost of video refresh (10% to 50% of cycles lost to the processor) as a number to design against |
| Descriptor ring (chapter 5) | Plan 9's `/dev/bitblt` and `/dev/draw` are command streams written to a device: a letter, then binary parameters, low-order byte first, with ids naming server-side bitmaps. A ring of descriptors that name framebuffer rectangles and operations is the same shape (my inference; no source says so). The Blit wire protocol shows what is needed when the link is unreliable (type, channel, CRC, acknowledgement every 64 bytes), and that the bus needs none of it | **Consistent**; no change needed |
| Remote display | In the Blit nothing graphical crosses the wire; in 8½ drawing commands cross it. If a remote pdp64 terminal is ever wanted (the manual says none), the Plan 9 route (device files served over a network; bitmaps stay on the terminal) is the one with sources, and costs a file protocol (9P), not new device registers | **Not a chapter-3 matter**; recorded for chapter 5 |

### I.9 Proposed edits to `manual/03-graphics.md` (proposals only; the chapter is not edited)

1. **3.1.1 and the Commentary, "By the author's account the Blit was a Motorola 68000 machine that received code and pixels...":** replace "code and pixels" with "code and text"; the sources (Pike 1983, Pike, Locanthi and Reiser 1985) say the terminal drew all pixels itself and the serial line carried downloaded programs, characters and editing messages. Keep "Motorola 68000" (confirmed: 8 MHz MC68000, 256 K dual-ported RAM, 800×1024×1 display).
2. **The same sentence, "a precursor of the X11 protocol and of the NeWS window system":** mark as the author's account; none of the primary or secondary sources read says it. If wanted, replace by the confirmed lineage: Blit (`mpx`, `mux`), then 8½, then rio (Pike: 8½ is "similar to those of mux"); and the confirmed similarity that NeWS and the Blit both download code into the display so the interaction loop runs locally.
3. **The Commentary's "a framebuffer in the address space plus a bit-block-transfer engine":** the Blit had the framebuffer in the address space but **no** blit engine ("no pixel-moving hardware"; `bitblt` was software). Say, in effect: the Blit's framebuffer and its bit-block-transfer operation, which the Blit did in software; the pdp64 adds the engine. Same fix in the glossary (`appendix-c-glossary.md`, entry "Blit") and `01-introduction.md` if it repeats the engine wording.
4. **3.1.4 BLT64:** add the design rule from the Blit authors, "make the hardware transparent, so it can be bypassed" (the framebuffer stays plain memory, so software can draw without the blitter), and say how many raster operations are required (four were enough on the Blit: STORE, OR, CLR, XOR) and which are optional.
5. **3.1.2 video device:** add that the Blit read its mouse position registers at the vertical-retrace interrupt and drew its cursor in software in XOR; say whether the pdp64 video device has a cursor or sprite, and a vblank interrupt (14F proposed the latter).
6. **3.1.1 Precedent paragraph:** add the Blit's stated trade-off (the cost of a slow link and of splitting a program in two) as the reason the pdp64's terminal is on the bus, and add the Plan 9 note that its window system moved into files because that made it small and usable remotely.
7. **3.2.1 stub:** the Blit "operations and windowing model" the stub says are to be supplied by the author can be taken from Tradeoffs 1985 (the four primitives `bitblt`, `texture`, `segment`, `point`; the `Bitmap`, `Rectangle`, `Point`, `Texture` data types; STORE, OR, CLR, XOR; rectangles half-open) and Layers 1983 (windows that overlap, obscured parts kept off-screen).

### I.10 Pace and failures in round 6

Hosts used: doc.cat-v.org (11 pages, 3 file downloads), man.cat-v.org (4), www.cs.tufts.edu (1 page, 2 file requests), 9p.io (2 pages, 2 file requests), archive.org (3: two search calls and the `_djvu.txt` attempt, 65 s or more apart), en.wikipedia.org (4), www.tuhs.org (1 index, 9 messages), html.duckduckgo.com (4). One 403 (the Tradeoffs PDF on the Tufts server: a file permission, not a rate limit), one 401 (archive.org journal scan), one 404 (a guessed path). No 429, 503, captcha or block page.

## 14J. Corrections and clarifications from round 6

The author asked round 6 to go back over earlier statements. The working list is [`verification-ledger.tsv`](codegen-data/kernelpain/verification-ledger.tsv) (every flagged row, with a status). This section lists **only what changed**; everything marked VERIFIED in the ledger needed no change. Statuses used: VERIFIED (what was checked), CORRECTED (old text, new text), CLARIFIED (ambiguous wording fixed), STILL UNVERIFIED (why).

**Checks run over all three documents**

* **Quotation gate over every double-quoted string of 30 or more characters** (993 strings, in the whole text of the three documents, compared, ignoring punctuation and case, with the saved pages, the Handbook OCR, the local kernel trees, the 4.3BSD source and all of `docs/*.md`, `docs/orig/*.md` and `docs/manual/*.md`). Result: 5 failures, all corrected or explained below (C1 to C3 and the OCR note).
* **Section references.** Every "(N.N.N†)" reference in chapter 0 (24 distinct) was resolved against `docs/manual/`: all 24 exist and say what the row says. The seven "decision" rows of 14F (3.1.2 to 3.5) also resolve.
* **Attribution check by program.** For the 320 statement rows that have a quotation, the quotation was located in a saved page and the names and years in the "Who, when" cell were searched for in the same page. 25 rows had a name or year not in that page; checked by hand: 13 are rows with two sources (the second person or date is in another saved page of the same thread, or the name is spelt with an accent in the page), 5 are Keith Packard's blog posts (the author is the blog's owner and the saved text has no byline), 6 are round-6 rows whose date is that of the paper or manual, not of the quoted page, and 1 was a real error (C5).

**Corrections (CORRECTED), in the order of how much the pdp64 design leans on them**

| # | Where | Old text | New text | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| C4 | 14F "Remote display lineage" (Blit row), coverage matrix | Blit paper "not read"; 'Nothing here says the Blit was an m68k receiving code and pixels over a serial link' | superseded by 14I.7: 68000, serial link and downloaded code **confirmed**; "pixels" and "bit-block-transfer engine" **contradicted**; X11 and NeWS precursor **unconfirmed** | the papers were read in round 6 |

## 14K. Round 6: two thin matrix rows worked (interrupt trigger types, BSD graphics)

Both rows come from files that are saved or local; labels as in 14F ([D-local] the kernel tree, [M] manual page).

| Pain | What was said | Who, when | Source | Label | pdp64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Edge interrupts can arrive while the handler runs, and not every controller can re-trigger one | "Interrupt occurs on the falling and/or rising edge of a hardware signal. The occurrence is latched into the irq controller hardware and must be acked in order to be reenabled. After the ack another interrupt can happen on the same source even before the first one is handled by the associated event handler."; the generic code keeps a loop and a pending flag for it (`handle_edge_irq`: `IRQS_PENDING`, "mask_ack_irq"); moving the interrupt to another core needs a busy wait on the in-progress flag, or "for edge type interrupts the handler might get stuck on the previous target"; and "Not all irq controller chips can retrigger interrupts at the hardware level, so in those cases we allow the resending of IRQs via a tasklet." | Linus Torvalds, Ingo Molnar and Thomas Gleixner (copyright lines of `kernel/irq/resend.c`, 1992-2006); the `handle_edge_irq` comment, undated | `kernel/irq/chip.c`, `kernel/irq/resend.c` | [D-local] | **UNKNOWN**: 5.7.1 puts the cause in an `IRQ` register where "each bit is cleared by writing a 1", a latched cause, so a device cannot lose an edge between two reads; the manual does not say whether the line itself is level or edge, or how an interrupt moves between cores (7.8.3 leaves routing undefined, decision R1) |
| The BSD ports of the Linux graphics drivers | OpenBSD: "Support is currently available for the following graphics cards: amdgpu(4) ... intel(4) ... radeon(4)"; "The drm driver first appeared in OpenBSD 4.3."; the page does not say the drivers come from Linux and names no NVIDIA part. FreeBSD's port of the Linux drivers was confirmed in round 5 (status report) | OpenBSD manual page `drm(4)` (page dated 7 Jan 2022) | `man.openbsd.org/drm.4` | [M] | manual 3.5.3: the OpenBSD half is **partly confirmed** (AMD and Intel only, 14J); a BSD that wants a PCI Express graphics card carries a driver for each vendor, which is the burden the on-bus terminal of 3.1 does not have |

## 15. What this means for pdp64

Ranked by the author's three principles. "Shows up as" says where the item appears in a kernel: recovery code (error paths and fix-ups), hot-path length (instructions on a path that runs constantly), or code size.

**Where the design wins (the pain does not arise).**

| Rank | What | Principle | Shows up as | Evidence in this document |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sequential consistency and natural alignment: no barrier vocabulary, no Alpha case, no split-lock atomics, no unaligned fix-up | (a), (b) | code size and recovery code: the ordering vocabulary is 5,596 Linux code lines in 855 files plus 3,016 lines of `memory-barriers.txt`; the alignment trap handler and `get_unaligned` family | chapters 6, 11 |
| 2 | Registers-only exception entry, banked `SP`/`DP`, `pushx`/`pushm`: entry cannot fault, no `swapgs`-like trick, 4 to 5 instruction entry | (b), (c) | hot-path length (45 to 70 instructions on RISC and x86) and recovery code (the paranoid entry path, the double-fault path) | chapter 3 |
| 3 | No FPU or vector state, no 32-bit mode, one byte order: lazy-FPU, `xsave`, signal-frame sizing, compat layer, endian helpers | (a), (b) | code size: `arch/x86/kernel/fpu` 4,364 lines, `compat` 591 code lines | chapters 8, 11 |
| 4 | Precise, restartable faults with no architectural effect, bounded fault matrix: no `backup()` | (b) | recovery code: V7's decode-the-instruction routine, ARM-style abort fix-ups | chapter 14 |
| 5 | Broadcast `TLBI` with `TLBSYNC`, ASIDs, `G` pages: no shootdown protocol, no switch flush | (b), (c) | recovery code (shootdown races) and hot-path length (switch) | chapter 5 |
| 6 | No speculation: none of the 4,164 lines of `hw-vuln` documents, no model blacklist | (a), (b) | code size and boot-time selection logic | chapter 4 |
| 7 | Coherent CPUs, uncached DMA mapping: no cache-maintenance hooks for DMA | (a), (b) | code size: 131 files mention the flush family | chapter 7 |

**Where it is exposed, ranked by what I think it will cost.**

| Rank | Exposure | Principle hurt | Shows up as | Pointer |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **The single page-table base and kernel mapped in every address space, if the core ever speculates** (E38 keeps that door open) | (b), (c) | a PTI-style switch on every entry and exit: Linux's own documentation puts a `cr3` write at "on the order of a hundred cycles" at each entry and exit, and the engineer who wrote the patches reported "a few hundred cycles" per syscall or interrupt, about 5% typical and about 30% worst case (Hansen, 31 Oct 2017, section 14A, [D]); the design cost is a second base register now, versus a mitigation later | chapter 4; D6 |
| 2 | **Software accessed and dirty bits** | (c) | an extra fault per first touch and per first write of each page; Linux's arm64 default is hardware management to avoid it. Offsetting benefit: less recovery code, no walker writes | chapter 5; Q2 |
| 3 | **Uncached DMA buffers** | (c) | CPU reads of received data go to the bus; needs P6 the day a non-coherent bus master exists | chapter 7; P6, R5, R6 |
| 4 | **Machine check halts** | (b) in the broad sense | one uncorrectable memory error stops the system; Linux spends 7,111 lines on x86 alone to avoid that | chapter 13 |
| 5 | **Fault recovery in the copy routines has no mechanism yet** (exception table, `nofault` or probe) | (b) | the one place the kernel must have recovery code and the documents are silent | chapter 9 |
| 6 | **TLB invalidation granularity**: one page or all, no by-ASID, no range | (c) | N broadcasts to tear down a mapping; rollover handling | chapter 5; P5 |
| 7 | **Cross-modifying code and page-size changes have no rules** | (b) | iTLB-multihit-class and patching races | chapters 4, 12; E39 item 7 |
| 8 | **Lock scalability beyond 2 to 4 cores, interrupt controller, IPIs, virtualization, hardware breakpoints, performance counters** | all | not yet faced | R1, R2, R3, R8, P16 |
| 9 | **39-bit address space**: KASLR and ASLR entropy | security, not a principle | fewer bits to randomise | chapters 5, 12 |

**See also:** the rules that keep pdp64 free of the speculation and side-channel flaws are in [`hardware-vulnerabilities.md`](hardware-vulnerabilities.md); OpenBSD's mitigations against pdp64, with the improvements an OpenBSD developer would want, are in [`openbsd-mitigations.md`](openbsd-mitigations.md).

**Candidate actions suggested by the evidence** (none is decided; each cites the decision that would carry it): write the "visible only after `TLBI`" and the page-size-change rules into the ISA (E39 item 7) before any second core; decide whether a second page-table base is cheap enough to reserve now (chapter 4); state the cache-index-in-page-offset requirement for any future data cache; decide the fault-fixup mechanism for copies (chapter 9), because it is the only mandatory recovery code; keep P5 (by-ASID invalidate) and P1 (wake rule) on the list; keep hardware A/D management as a later option behind a feature register (P4) so that software can stay faulting-only if desired.

## 16. What this does not show

* **What we did to lwn.net.** The browser queue database records 68 completed lwn.net directives between 13:02 and 14:20 on 2026-10-22; 4 of them (13:02 to 13:03) belong to an earlier verification run and **64 are mine, from 14:07:07 to 14:20:40, 49 of them after the author's 14:10 pause** (they had been queued as one batch before the pause reached me, which was the wrong way to queue). Only 6 articles and the Kernel index returned real pages; the others returned the site's \"Blocked due to excessive requests\" page or failed after the browser window was closed. After the block, no request went to lwn.net; the LWN citations in this document are from those seven pages.
* **Message counts have false positives, and here is the biggest one found.** A first run of the commit-message mining matched `spectre` in laptop speaker-quirk commits (HP Spectre) and `mds` in PowerPC MDS evaluation-board commits; the expressions were tightened and the mining re-run (Appendix B.4, B.7). Other categories keep known noise (`mitigation` includes thermal mitigation, `poison` includes slab poisoning, `dma` includes graphics buffers). Treat counts as where words concentrate.
* **The history is partly missing.** The kernel.org `history.git` URL (meant for 1991 to 2005) holds the same 2005 to 2010 commits as the main repository and only 31 earlier ones, so Linux before 2005 has no commit-message data; the BSDs begin in 1993 to 1995. The mailing-list reading is a sample of about twenty primary pages, chosen by me, not a survey of thirty years of lists.
* **Round 2 is a sample chosen by queries, not a survey.** About 340 pages were fetched (319 unique URLs; the index is `round2-index.tsv`), selected as the first one to three distinct hits per query, so routine mail is included (206 of the pages are marked routine or context, 7 are failed pages). The statements in 14B and A.2 are those I read and judged to carry a cost, a decision or an admission; threads I did not read may contradict them. Some replies are quotations of others; the table names the author of the page that was read.
* **LWN was read only through archived copies.** Eight LWN articles came through the Wayback Machine (several of the archived 2024 captures were LWN's own "Blocked due to excessive requests" page, so an earlier year was used); the others in Appendix C are not fetched. Direct requests to lwn.net were never repeated after the block. archive.org returned 429 once on 22 October after about three requests and was paced at 45 to 60 seconds afterwards.
* **Old mailing lists are barely touched.** marc.info's search shows only the 30 newest hits, so it cannot reach mail before 2005; `lkml.iu.edu` (the Indiana University hypermail archive, 1996 to 2005) works, and 36 pages from twelve slices of it were read, chosen by subject keywords; they hold few statements about hardware cost. Nothing was read from the 1990s lists of the BSDs.
* **The OpenBSD list search is noisy.** marc.info body search returns recent mails that quote `dmesg` lines (so "meltdown" finds unrelated bug reports); 56 of the 81 OpenBSD pages are routine. The useful OpenBSD posts were found by following links from known ones.
* **Counts are vocabulary, not pain.** A word that appears often in current code or commit messages shows where developers talk about a thing, not how much it costs. "Race" and "deadlock" appear where fixes happened and where comments warn. The BSD kernels use different names for the same things (`membar_*`, `atomic_*`, `pmap_*`, `smr`), so cross-kernel comparisons of the barrier and RCU rows are not like-for-like.
* **The sample is partial.** The Linux sample has 91 directories: all `arch/` for eleven architectures, but `drivers` only for `irqchip` and `iommu`, and no `fs`. Categories dominated by drivers (DMA, interrupts, virtualization, MMIO) are undercounts. The BSD trees contain `sys/kern`, `sys/vm` or `uvm`, `sys/sys` and the listed architecture directories. Line counts use regular expressions over text, with no parsing; comments and code are not separated.
* **Current sources hide the past.** A workaround for hardware nobody sells any more has usually been deleted; the current tree shows pains still alive, not the ones that shaped design. The history is Phase 2 (commit messages, lists, articles).
* **No performance was measured.** All costs quoted are the developers' (documentation statements and, in Phase 2, list posts) or are path-length estimates from the survey; none was measured by me.
* **pdp64 verdicts are from documents.** There is no silicon, no SMP, no DMA devices, no interrupt controller and no kernel yet; "DESIGNED AWAY" means "cannot arise given the documented design", and several of those rest on the machine remaining in-order and non-speculative.
* **Memory is not evidence.** Items from memory (papers, who said what, dates) are marked unverified and listed in Appendix C until fetched.

## Appendix A: the pain-point catalogue

Columns: category; pain point; era (as far as the local evidence says, otherwise *from memory, unverified*); evidence with label; usual workaround; architectures that feel it most; pdp64 status; verdict. Decision identifiers refer to [`decisions-pending.md`](decisions-pending.md).

| # | Category | Pain point | Era | Evidence | Workaround | Architectures | pdp64 status | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Entry | Page-table switch at every kernel entry and exit | 2018 on (unverified; Meltdown) | `pti.rst` "on the order of a hundred cycles" [G] | PCID, trampolines, deferred user flush | x86, some arm64 | one base, no PTI now | UNKNOWN (SAME PROBLEM if speculative) |
| 2 | Entry | Kernel base register swap (`swapgs`) must nest perfectly | x86-64 era | `entry_64.rst` [G] | cheap/expensive mode tests | x86-64 | banked `DP` | DESIGNED AWAY |
| 3 | Entry | Entry code must not be traced or breakpointed | 2010s (unverified) | `core-api/entry.rst` `noinstr` [G] | special sections | all | none needed | MITIGATED BY DESIGN |
| 4 | Entry | Register save/restore length | always | survey 8 (estimated) | multiple-register save | RISC, x86 | 4 to 5 instructions | MITIGATED BY DESIGN |
| 5 | Entry | Entry can fault (bad stack, double fault) | x86 | system design D1 [G] | task-state-segment stacks, IST | x86 | register-only entry | DESIGNED AWAY |

## Appendix B: the counts, the method and the commands

### B.1 The trees used (measurement 1: the current sources)

Shallow, sparse checkouts under `~/claude/kernel-src` (not in this repository), read in place. Which directories are present is recorded in `docs/codegen-data/kernelpain/trees.csv` (`python3 tools/kernelpain.py trees`).

| Tree | Commit | Date | Sparse directories |
|---|---|---|---:|
| linux | `7be4bc52fd9e0e55dc9a2e0232b89427d2c4b614` | 2026-10-21 | 91 |
| freebsd | `3200a274272d28cf6d2385a8ea46959f313866de` | 2026-10-22 | 12 |
| netbsd | `cdcd52a8183344cabc6a4296b4bbe7e7c5fd3cdc` | 2026-10-21 | 32 |
| openbsd | `2114d7b7c8fbfd8d285dd7b5b5eb44e1f6b70f55` | 2026-10-22 | 18 |

Directories added for this study, one tree at a time: Linux `Documentation mm kernel include/linux include/asm-generic block net/core drivers/irqchip drivers/iommu virt lib`; the BSDs `sys/kern`, `sys/vm` or `sys/uvm`, `sys/sys`, `share/man/man9`.

### B.2 The method

`python3 tools/kernelpain.py grep` runs, for every category in B.4 and every tree, `nice -n 10 grep -rPIc [-i] --exclude-dir=.git -e <regex> .` in the tree root. Each output line is a file and the number of its lines that match. A file's area is its architecture directory (`arch/<family>`, with `i386`, `x86_64`, `amd64` merged into `x86`, `ppc`/`ppc64` into `powerpc`, `sparc64` into `sparc`, and so on, table `ARCH_FAMILY` in the script) or its top-level subsystem directory; its kind is `doc` (under `Documentation/`, in a `man` directory, or ending `.rst .txt .md .9`), `kconfig` (name starting `Kconfig`) or `code` (`.c .h .S .s .rs .asm .inc`). Line counts of code per area come from reading every such file (`totals.csv`). Output: `counts.csv` (tree, category, area, kind, files, lines) and `totals.csv`. `python3 tools/kernelpain.py structural` runs the named commands of B.3. `python3 tools/kernelpain.py report` prints the tables of B.5.

### B.4 The category expressions

| Category | Regular expression (PCRE, case-insensitive unless noted) |
|---|---|
| errata | `\berrat(?:um\|a)\b` |
| workaround | `\bwork-?arounds?\b` |
| quirk | `\bquirks?\b` |
| mitigation | `\bmitigat(?:e\|es\|ed\|ion\|ions\|ing)\b` |
| spectre-family | `\b(?:spectre(?!\s+(?:x360\|x2\|xt\|13\|15\|envy\|laptop\|speaker))\|meltdown\|retpolines?\|ibrs\|ibpb\|stibp\|ssbd\|retbleed\|zenbleed\|srso\|spec_ctrl\|speculative store bypass\|branch history injection\|gather data sampling\|rfds)\b` |
| mds-l1tf-taa | `(?:microarchitectural data sampling\|\bmds_[a-z_]+\|\bmds (?:mitigation\|vulnerab\w*\|clear\w*\|buffer\w*)\|\bl1tf\b\|l1 terminal fault\|foreshadow\|zombieload\|\btaa\b\|tsx async abort\|\bsrbds\b\|mmio stale data)` |
| speculation-any | `\bspeculat(?:ion\|ive\|ively\|e)\b` |
| kpti-entry | `\b(?:kpti\|pti\|page[- ]table isolation\|kaiser\|syscall entry\|sysenter\|sysret\|swapgs\|vsyscall\|entry trampoline\|entry code\|int 0x80\|ia32 syscall)\b` |
| hardening | `(?:kaslr\|aslr\|clang cfi\|kcfi\|fineibt\|control.flow integrity\|bti\|cet\|shadow stack\|stack protector\|stackprotector\|stack canary\|pointer authentication\|stack clash\|vmap stack\|init_on_alloc\|usercopy\|kasan\|kfence\|memory tagging\|rodata\|w\^x\|set_memory_ro\|fortify_source)\b` |
| barrier-ordering | `\b(?:memory barriers?\|smp_mb\|smp_rmb\|smp_wmb\|smp_read_barrier_depends\|read_barrier_depends\|dma_wmb\|dma_rmb\|memory ordering\|memory-ordering\|acquire/release\|load-acquire\|store-release\|READ_ONCE\|WRITE_ONCE\|mmiowb)\b` |
| race | `\bra(?:ce\|ces\|cy)\b(?! condition in the tracing)` |
| deadlock | `\b(?:deadlocks?\|livelocks?\|lockup\|lock-up\|hung task)\b` |
| lockdep | `\blockdep\b` |
| spinlock-family | `\b(?:spinlocks?\|ticket lock\|qspinlock\|queued spinlock\|mcs lock\|rwlocks?\|seqlocks?\|big kernel lock\|bkl\|lock_kernel\|lock contention\|lock scalability)\b` |
| rcu | `\b(?:rcu\|srcu\|call_rcu\|synchronize_rcu\|read-copy)\b` |
| atomics-llsc | `\b(?:cmpxchg\|ll/sc\|load-linked\|store-conditional\|ldrex\|strex\|lr/sc\|test_and_set\|atomic_cmpxchg\|compare-and-swap\|compare and swap)\b` |
| percpu | `\b(?:per-cpu\|percpu\|this_cpu\|cpu-local)\b` |
| tlb | `\b(?:tlbs?\|translation lookaside\|invlpg\|tlb flush)\b` |
| shootdown | `\b(?:shootdowns?\|shoot-downs?\|flush_tlb_others\|tlb flush ipi)\b` |
| asid-pcid | `\b(?:asids?\|invpcid\|vmids?)\b\|\bpcid\b(?!\s*header)` |
| pagetable | `\b(?:page tables?\|pgd\|pmd\|pud\|p4d\|five-level\|5-level paging\|4-level paging\|page walk\|page-walk\|pgtable)\b` |
| accessed-dirty | `\b(?:accessed bit\|dirty bit\|young bit\|referenced bit\|pte_young\|pte_dirty\|pte_mkyoung\|access flag\|soft-dirty\|software dirty\|dirty bit management\|hardware dirty\|hw dirty\|reference bit)\b` |
| huge-pages | `\b(?:transparent huge\|thp\|hugepages?\|huge pages?\|hugetlb)\b` |
| numa | `\b(?:numa\|mempolicy\|numa balancing)\b` |
| cow-fork-mmaplock | `\b(?:copy-on-write\|dup_mmap\|mmap_sem\|mmap_lock\|mmap lock\|per-vma lock\|vma lock)\b` |
| cache-coherence | `\b(?:cache coheren\w*\|cache alias\w*\|cache flush\w*\|cache invalidat\w*\|cache maintenance\|dcache\|icache\|d-cache\|i-cache\|non-?coherent\|vipt\|vivt\|writeback cache\|write-back cache\|cache line bounc\w*\|false sharing)\b` |
| dma | `\b(?:dma\|swiotlb\|dma-mapping\|iommu\|bounce buffers?)\b` |
| mmio-ordering | `\b(?:write[- ]combin\w*\|posted writes?\|mmiowb\|readl_relaxed\|writel_relaxed\|doorbell\|wmb\(\))` |
| write-buffer-delay-slot | `\b(?:write buffer\|store buffer\|delay slots?\|branch delay)\b` |
| tlb-refill | `\b(?:tlb refill\|tlb miss\|tlb exception\|tlb handler\|tlb load)\b` |
| fpu-vector-state | `\b(?:fpu\|xsave\w*\|xrstor\w*\|lazy fpu\|eager fpu\|fxsave\|avx\d*\|simd\|neon\|sve\|altivec\|vfp\|vector state\|floating[- ]point state)\b` |
| alignment | `\b(?:unaligned\|misaligned\|alignment fault\|alignment exception\|get_unaligned\|put_unaligned\|packed struct\|__packed)\b` |
| endian | `\b(?:endian\w*\|byte-?order\|byteswap\w*\|cpu_to_le\w*\|le32_to_cpu\|be32_to_cpu)\b` |
| compat-32bit | `\b(?:compat\|ia32 emulation\|compat_ioctl\|ilp32\|x32\|31-bit\|32-bit compat\w*)\b` |
| y2038-time | `\b(?:y2038\|year 2038\|time_t\|timespec64\|64-bit time\|2038)\b` |
| timekeeping | `\b(?:vdso\|tsc\|clocksource\|timekeeping\|clock_gettime\|hpet\|time stamp counter\|monotonic clock)\b` |
| interrupts | `\b(?:interrupt latency\|irq latency\|irq storm\|interrupt storm\|spurious (?:irq\|interrupt)\w*\|eoi\|msi-x\|msi\|irq affinity\|apic\|gic\|plic\|ipi\|ipis)\b` |
| timers-tick | `\b(?:nohz\|tickless\|dynticks\|hrtimer\|timer wheel\|timer interrupt\|tick broadcast\|jiffies)\b` |
| idle-power | `\b(?:cpuidle\|mwait\|hlt\|wfi\|c-state\|c-states\|cpufreq\|runtime pm\|power domain\|suspend to ram\|hibernat\w*)\b` |
| virtualization | `\b(?:kvm\|nested virtualization\|nested paging\|vmexit\|vm exit\|hypercalls?\|paravirt\w*\|stage-2\|virtio\|xen)\b` |
| uaccess | `\b(?:copy_from_user\|copy_to_user\|uaccess\|smap\|smep\|get_user\|put_user\|strncpy_from_user\|exception tables?\|__ex_table\|stac\|clac)\b` |
| tracing-patching | `\b(?:ftrace\|kprobes?\|uprobes?\|text_poke\|int3\|jump labels?\|static keys?\|static calls?\|self-modifying\|kgdb\|hw_breakpoint\|hardware breakpoint\|single-step\w*)\b` |
| machine-check-poison | `\b(?:machine checks?\|mce\|hwpoison\|memory failure\|poisoned\|poison\|uncorrectable\|edac\|ecc error\w*)\b` |
| net-storage-fastpath | `\b(?:napi\|xdp\|zero-copy\|zerocopy\|busy[- ]poll\w*\|io_uring\|af_xdp\|gro\|tso\|batching\|lockless\|lock-free)\b` |
| marker-xxx-fixme-hack | `\b(?:XXX\|FIXME\|HACK\|KLUDGE)\b` | (case-sensitive)
| hardware-is-broken | `\b(?:(?:hardware\|cpu\|chip\|silicon\|firmware\|bios\|hw) (?:bug\|bugs\|quirk\|erratum\|errata\|brokenness)\|broken (?:hardware\|hw\|firmware\|bios\|cpus?)\|buggy (?:hardware\|hw\|cpus?\|firmware\|bios\|devices?))\b` |

### B.5 The per-category and per-architecture tables (the "table dump")

Counts are matching lines (not occurrences). "code lines" and "code files" are in files of kind code, "doc lines" and "kconfig lines" in the other kinds. The per-architecture tables give lines per 1,000 lines of code under that directory (the denominator counts only the sparse sample). Source: `docs/codegen-data/kernelpain/counts.csv`, `totals.csv`.

### B.6 Measurement 2: commit messages on treeless bare clones

`tools/kernelpain-msgs.sh` clones one repository at a time with `git clone --bare --filter=tree:0 --single-branch <url>` (commits only: no trees, no blobs, no checkout), runs `git log --no-merges --date=format:%Y-%m-%d --format=<hash, date, subject, body>` with no path arguments, classifies each message with the B.4 expressions, writes CSV files `docs/codegen-data/kernelpain/msgs-<repo>-*.csv`, and deletes the clone. Per-subsystem and per-architecture counts come from the subject prefix (the text before the first colon, for example `x86/mm`, `arm64`, `mm`), per-year counts from the commit date. The results are summarised in B.7 when they exist.

Four repositories were mined on 2026-10-22 with `tools/kernelpain-msgs.sh linux freebsd netbsd openbsd` (treeless bare clones, deleted afterwards): Linux (torvalds/linux, 1,377,457 non-merge commits from 2005, head `a1c2b1da93f5`), FreeBSD (309,283 from 1993, head `775e421dc38f`), NetBSD (317,170 from 1993, head `5b0e0d599751`) and OpenBSD (248,109 from 1995, head `d962f40cd0b4`). The exact `git log` command and head hash of each is in `docs/codegen-data/kernelpain/msgs-<repo>-meta.csv`. The counts say how often a pain word appears in a commit's subject or message; they do not say how much pain there was (section 16). Known noise: before 2018 nothing in `spectre-family` should match (the laptop-speaker commits were removed by the second run); `mds-l1tf-taa` still matches Ceph's "mds" (metadata server) and Staging drivers, so its counts are inflated and only its trend is usable.

**What the shape shows** (per 1000 non-merge commits of the period, from the tables below. Linux's `-1999` and `2000-04` columns hold 2 and 32 commits and are meaningless; the first usable Linux period is 2005-09. The BSDs begin in 1993 to 1995):

* **Hardware-defect words are flat; mitigation and hardening words grew.** In Linux `errata` stays at 1.4 to 1.8, `workaround` at 5.7 to 6.2 and `quirk` at 5.5 to 8.2 across 2005 to 2026, while `mitigation` rises from 0.4 to 2.3 and `hardening` from 0.9-1.5 to 10.9. FreeBSD shows the same: `mitigation` 0.1 to 1.7, `hardening` 0.2 to 4.9. The cost of hardware imperfection did not fall; the new cost is defence.
* **Concurrency words grew most.** `rcu` 4.6 to 9.9, `atomics-llsc` 1.4 to 5.6, `barrier-ordering` 1.4 to 3.7, `percpu` 4.1 to 5.2 in Linux; `spinlock-family` fell from 9.8 to 5.9 (the move from locks to lock-free and RCU); OpenBSD's `spinlock-family` rose from 0.5 to 3.1 and its `interrupts` from 0.1 to 3.4 in the same years as its multiprocessor work.
* **Speculation is a 2018 event.** `spectre-family` is zero until 2015-19 and 1.2 to 1.3 per thousand after; its first Linux match is a retpoline commit of 11 Jan 2018 (`2641f08bb7fc`). `kpti-entry` goes 1.3 to 4.0. The BSDs show the same jump in the same period (2015-19: FreeBSD 0.6, NetBSD 0.8, OpenBSD 1.0, Linux 1.3), then a fall in 2020-26 to 0.1 to 0.6 against Linux's 1.2.
* **Things that faded:** `tlb-refill` (software-refilled TLBs) 0.4 to 0.1, `endian` 7.0 to 3.0 in Linux, `write-buffer-delay-slot` flat at 0.3, `compat-32bit` 3.9 to 2.5, and `y2038-time` peaks in 2015-19 (1.8) after the conversion started.
* **A BSD-specific growth:** FreeBSD's `compat-32bit` rose from 2.0 to 10.9 per thousand (32-bit and Linux emulation layers), NetBSD's from 3.4 to 7.7, OpenBSD's stayed 1 to 3: a compatibility layer is a cost that keeps growing where it exists.
* **Things that grew with scale:** `huge-pages` 1.9 to 5.0, `net-storage-fastpath` 4.4 to 16.5, `virtualization` 19 to 33, `fpu-vector-state` 1.8 to 3.3-3.9.
* **Where the quirk and workaround words concentrate by subject prefix** (second table of each repository): device subsystems, not CPU architectures. In Linux the highest rates are `alsa` 71, `hid` 58, `acpi` 32, `pci` 32 and `mmc` 26 per 1000 commits, against `x86` 11, `arm64` 8 and `arm` 6. The pain of "the hardware does not follow its specification" is mostly in the long tail of peripherals.

### C.2 The plan, in priority order

The mailing lists are the main source (the author's wording: all kernel and operating-system development lists of the past thirty years). For each pain point: what it is, who felt it and when, the workaround, the hot instruction sequence, what a developer said it cost.

| Priority | Source and query | Expected to teach | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | lore.kernel.org search: `PTI`, `KAISER`, `retpoline`, `Meltdown`, `swapgs`, 2017 and 2018 (LKML, x86) | the cost developers measured for entry and exit mitigations, who objected | partly done: Hansen's KAISER cover letter, Torvalds on speculation, Lutomirski on PCID read; the 2017-18 patch threads on `swapgs`/entry, `IBRS` costs and the per-process switch not yet | 
| 2 | lore.kernel.org: Paul McKenney and Will Deacon on `memory-barriers`, `Alpha`, `READ_ONCE`, `dependency ordering`, `smp_read_barrier_depends` removal (linux-arch, LKML) | the ordering pain in the architects' words | not yet |
| 3 | lore.kernel.org: Linus Torvalds on `ticket spinlock`, `qspinlock`, `cmpxchg`, `LL/SC`, `unaligned`, `lazy FPU`, `LazyFP`, `SMAP` | design-level objections and costs | not yet |
